is constant on the elements of
{\mathcal{P}}
.

Let us give the following definition.

Definition 4.13
.

Let
(\Omega,\bar{\mathfrak{B}},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space with
\bar{\mathfrak{B}}
a finite algebra and
{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{\mathfrak{B}})
the associated unique minimal partition (
4.19
).
Given
m:=\#{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{\mathfrak{B}})
, we define the probability space

(\Omega^{m},\mathcal{S}(\Omega^{m}),\mathbb{P}^{m})
, where
\Omega^{m}:=\{1,\dots,m\}
,
\mathcal{S}(\Omega^{m})
is the algebra generated by
(\{1\},\ldots\{m\})
and
\mathbb{P}^{m}(\{k\}):=\mathbb{P}(A_{k})
,
k=1,\dots,m
.

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
\mathbb{S}:=(U,f,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1
.
Let
(\Omega,\bar{\mathfrak{B}},\mathbb{P})
and
(\Omega^{m},\mathcal{S}(\Omega^{m}),\mathbb{P}^{m})
as in Definition
4.13
.
Then the Lagrangian problems
\textup{{L}}_{\bar{\mathfrak{B}}}:=\textup{{L}}(\Omega,\bar{\mathfrak{B}},\mathbb{P})
and
\textup{{L}}^{m}:=\textup{{L}}(\Omega^{m},\mathcal{S}(\Omega^{m}),\mathbb{P}^{m})
are equivalent in the sense of Definition
4.4
.

Proof.

Let
\psi:\Omega\to\Omega^{m}
the function given by

\psi(\omega)=k,\quad\text{ if }\omega\in A_{k},\quad k=1,\dots,m

and
\phi:\Omega^{m}\to\Omega
defined by

\phi(k)=\omega_{k},\quad k=1,\dots,m,

for a fixed
\omega_{k}\in A_{k}
.
We prove that the maps
\psi
and
\phi
satisfy the assumptions of Proposition
4.6
. Measurability of the map
\psi
follows from the fact that
\psi^{-1}(\{k\})=A_{k}
for any
k=1,\dots,m
, while the measurability of
\phi
is trivial since
\Omega^{m}
is equipped with the algebra
\mathcal{S}(\Omega^{m})
. Moreover, it is immediate to verify that
\psi\circ\phi=i_{\Omega^{m}}
, which implies (
4.3
). We have to verify (
4.4
): given
X_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have that
X_{0}
is constant on the elements
A_{k}
of the partition
{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{\mathfrak{B}})
, hence it easily follows that
X_{0}=X_{0}\circ\phi\circ\psi
.
Let us verify that
\psi_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{P}^{m}
: for any
k=1,\dots,m
, we have
(\psi_{\sharp}\mathbb{P})(\{k\})=\mathbb{P}(\psi^{-1}(\{k\}))=\mathbb{P}(A_{k})=\mathbb{P}^{m}(\{k\})
. Finally, we verify that
\phi_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}^{m}=\mathbb{P}
:
for any measurable function
g:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
, recalling that
g
is piecewise constant on the elements of
{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{\mathfrak{B}})
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}g(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}(\phi_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}^{m})(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega^{m}}g(\phi(k))\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}^{m}(k)=\sum_{k=1}^{m}g(\omega_{k})\mathbb{P}(A_{k})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}g(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega).

∎

We recall the notation
{\mathcal{B}}_{[0,T]}
for the Borel
\sigma
-algebra on
[0,T]
.

Definition 4.15
.

[Finite Approximation Property]
Let
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space.
We say that the family of finite algebras
\mathfrak{B}^{n}\subset\mathfrak{B}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
satisfies the
finite approximation property
if
for any Banach space
E
and any
g\in L_{\mathbb{P}}^{1}(\Omega;E)
,
there exists a sequence
g^{n}:\Omega\to E
such that

(i)

g^{n}
is
\mathfrak{B}^{n}
-measurable for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
;

(ii)

g^{n}(\Omega)\subset\mathrm{\overline{co}}\left(g(\Omega)\right)
, where
\mathrm{\overline{co}}\left(g(\Omega)\right)

denotes the closed convex hull of
g(\Omega)
;

(iii)

\left\|g^{n}-g\right\|_{L_{\mathbb{P}}^{1}(\Omega;E)}\to 0
, as
n\to+\infty
;

(iv)

if
G:[0,T]\times\Omega\to E
is
({\mathcal{B}}_{[0,T]}\otimes\mathfrak{B})
-measurable
and
g_{t}(\cdot):=G(t,\cdot)\in L_{\mathbb{P}}^{1}(\Omega;E)
for any
t\in[0,T]
,
then the maps

G^{n}:[0,T]\times\Omega\to E
defined by
G^{n}(t,\omega):=g^{n}_{t}(\omega)
, where
g^{n}_{t}

is a sequence associated to
g_{t}
satisfying items
(i)
,
(ii)
,
(iii)
,
are
({\mathcal{B}}_{[0,T]}\otimes\mathfrak{B}^{n})
-measurable for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
be a standard Borel space.

(1)

Then there exists a family of finite algebras

\mathfrak{B}^{n}\subset\mathfrak{B}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, satisfying the finite approximation property of Definition
4.15
.

(2)

If
\mathbb{P}
is without atoms, then there exists a family
\mathfrak{B}^{n}\subset\mathfrak{B}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, satisfying the finite approximation property of Definition
4.15
such that the associated minimal partition
{\mathcal{P}}(\mathfrak{B}^{n})=\{A_{k}^{n}:k=1,\ldots,n\}
contains exactly
n
elements and

(4.20)

\mathbb{P}(A^{n}_{k})=\frac{1}{n},\qquad k=1,\ldots,n.

The proof of Proposition
4.16
is postponed in Appendix
D
.
Results similar to item (1) of Proposition
4.16
can be found in
[
52
, Theorem 6.1.12]
, where a martingale approach is employed.

Theorem 4.17

(Approximation by piecewise constant controls for
L
)
.

Let
\mathbb{S}:=(U,f,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1
with
U
a convex compact subset of a separable Banach space
V
.
Let
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space and assume that there exists
\{\mathfrak{B}^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfying the finite approximation property of Definition
4.15
.
Let
X_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
(X,u)\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})
.
If
\{X^{n}_{0}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfies
\|X^{n}_{0}-X_{0}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\to 0
,
then there exists a sequence
(X^{n},u^{n})\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{L}}}(X^{n}_{0})
such that

(1)

u^{n}
is
({\mathcal{B}}_{[0,T]}\otimes\mathfrak{B}^{n})
-measurable;

(2)

u^{n}\to u
in
({\mathcal{L}}_{T}\otimes\mathbb{P})
-measure, as
n\to+\infty
;

(3)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|X^{n}_{t}-X_{t}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\to 0
, as
n\to+\infty
;

(4)

J_{\textup{{L}}}(X^{n},u^{n})\to J_{\textup{{L}}}(X,u)
, as
n\to+\infty
.

Moreover, if
X_{0}^{n}
is
\mathfrak{B}^{n}
-measurable,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then

X_{t}^{n}
is
\mathfrak{B}^{n}
-measurable for any
t\in[0,T]
.

Proof.

For any
t\in[0,T]
, we denote with
u_{t}:\Omega\to U
the measurable control function
u
at time
t
.
For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in[0,T]
let
u^{n}_{t}:\Omega\to U
be the
\mathfrak{B}^{n}
-measurable approximation of
u_{t}
given by Definition
4.15
.
By the convexity of
U
and its compactness, from the property
(ii)
of Definition
4.15

it follows that
u^{n}_{t}(\Omega)\subset U
.
Defining
u^{n}(t,\omega):=u^{n}_{t}(\omega)
, thanks to the property
(iv)
of Definition
4.15
, we have that
u^{n}
is
({\mathcal{B}}_{[0,T]}\otimes\mathfrak{B}^{n})
-measurable.
By Proposition
4.8
we have the existence of
X^{n}
with
(X^{n},u^{n})\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{L}}}(X^{n}_{0})
.

From the compactness of
U
and the dominated convergence theorem it follows that

\|u^{n}-u\|_{L^{1}([0,T]\times\Omega;V)}\to 0
, as
n\to+\infty
.
Consequently, (2) holds.
Properties (3) and (4) follow by Proposition
4.11
.
∎

In the following, we reformulate the approximation result of Theorem
4.17
with the language of particles.
Let
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space and
\mathfrak{B}^{n}
a finite algebra,
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Denote with
{\mathcal{P}}(\mathfrak{B}^{n}):=\{A_{k}^{n}:k=1,\ldots,k(n)\}
the associated unique minimal partition and define
(\Omega^{k(n)},\mathcal{S}(\Omega^{k(n)}),\mathbb{P}^{k(n)})
by

(4.21)

\displaystyle\Omega^{k(n)}:=\{1,\dots,k(n)\},\quad\mathcal{S}(\Omega^{k(n)}):=\sigma(\{1\},\ldots\{k(n)\})

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{k(n)}(\{k\}):=\mathbb{P}(A^{n}_{k}),\quad k=1,\dots,{k(n)}.

In order to approximate trajectories, controls and costs of a Lagrangian problem
\textup{{L}}=\textup{{L}}(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
with the respective quantities in
\textup{{L}}^{k(n)}=\textup{{L}}(\Omega^{k(n)},\mathcal{S}(\Omega^{k(n)}),\mathbb{P}^{k(n)})
we introduce, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the maps
\psi^{n},\phi^{n}
and
{\mathcal{K}}^{n}
.
This is necessary due to the fact that the trajectories are not defined on the same space.

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote with
\psi^{n},\phi^{n}
the maps

(4.22)

\begin{split}&\psi^{n}:\Omega\to\Omega^{k(n)},\quad\psi^{n}(\omega)=k,\quad\text{ if }\omega\in A^{n}_{k},\quad k=1,\dots,k(n);\\
&\phi^{n}:\Omega^{k(n)}\to\Omega,\quad\phi^{n}(k)=\omega_{k}^{n},\quad k=1,\dots,k(n),\quad\text{for a fixed }\omega_{k}^{n}\in A^{n}_{k}.\end{split}

Moreover, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we introduce the map
{\mathcal{K}}^{n}:L^{p}(\Omega^{k(n)};C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times\mathrm{M}([0,T]\times\Omega^{k(n)};U)

\to L^{p}((\Omega,\mathfrak{B}^{n},\mathbb{P});C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times\mathrm{M}([0,T]\times\Omega;U)
given by

(4.23)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathcal{K}}^{n}(Y,v):=(Y\circ\psi^{n},\hat{v}),

\displaystyle\text{ where}\qquad\hat{v}(t,\omega):=v(t,\psi^{n}(\omega)),\quad\forall\,(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\Omega.

Proposition 4.18
.

Let
\mathbb{S}:=(U,f,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1
with
U
a convex compact subset of a separable Banach space
V
.
Let
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
and be a probability space and assume that there exists
\{\mathfrak{B}^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfying the finite approximation property of Definition
4.15
.
For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
(\Omega^{k(n)},\mathcal{S}(\Omega^{k(n)}),\mathbb{P}^{k(n)})
as in (
4.21
).

Let
X_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
(X,u)\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})
.
If
Y^{n}_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega^{k(n)};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
satisfies

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\|Y^{n}_{0}\circ\psi^{n}-X_{0}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}=0,

then there exists
a sequence
(Y^{n},v^{n})\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{L}}^{k(n)}}(Y^{n}_{0})
such that

(1)

{\mathcal{K}}^{n}(Y^{n},v^{n})\to(X,u)
in
C([0,T];L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times L^{1}([0,T]\times\Omega;V)
, as
n\to+\infty
;

(2)

J_{\textup{{L}}^{k(n)}}(Y^{n},v^{n})\to J_{\textup{{L}}}(X,u)
, as
n\to+\infty
.

Proof.

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, by Proposition
4.14
it holds that
\textup{{L}}_{\mathfrak{B}^{n}}\sim\textup{{L}}^{k(n)}
in the sense of Definition
4.4
.
Thanks to Proposition
A.3
we have that
X\in\mathrm{AC}^{p}([0,T];L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, hence we conclude applying
Theorem
4.17
.
Since
U
is compact, recall that the convergence
u^{n}\to u\in L^{1}([0,T]\times\Omega;V)
is equivalent to the convergence in
({\mathcal{L}}_{T}\otimes\mathbb{P})
-measure.
∎

Remark 4.19
.

If
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
is a standard Borel space, the existence of a sequence of finite algebras
\mathfrak{B}^{n}
is guaranteed by item (1) in Proposition
4.16
.
Moreover, if
\mathbb{P}
is without atoms, it is possible to choose
\mathfrak{B}^{n}
s.t.
\#\mathfrak{B}^{n}=n
and satisfying the property (
4.20
) given in item (2) in Proposition
4.16
.

Notice that the assumption
\mathbb{P}
without atoms is necessary to get a sequence of
\mathfrak{B}^{n}
with the property (
4.20
). Indeed, if there exists
\omega_{0}\in\Omega
such that
\mathbb{P}(\{\omega_{0}\})=\alpha>0
, then for
n\in\mathbb{N}
big enough the property (
4.20
) fails.

4.3.
Approximation by continuous controls and trajectories

The objective of the subsection is twofold.
In Proposition
4.21
, under continuity assumptions on both the initial datum and the control, we exhibit a stability result for a Lagrangian problem
\textup{{L}}(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
when
\mathbb{P}
is approximated by a sequence of probability measures
\mathbb{P}^{n}
.
Then, in Theorem
4.22
we approximate admissible controls with
continuous
controls so that the associated trajectories are continuous as well and
the associated costs converge.
These results are useful to prove the equivalence between Lagrangian and Eulerian optimal control problems (see the proof of Theorem
8.5
).

Throughout the section, we assume that

(4.24)

(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
standard Borel space,
\tau
a Polish topology on
\Omega
such that
\mathfrak{B}={\mathcal{B}}_{(\Omega,\tau)}
.

In the following regularity result, we prove the existence of a continuous trajectory for the Lagrangian dynamics whenever both the initial datum and the control are continuous.

Lemma 4.20

(Continuity)
.

Let
\mathbb{S}:=(U,f,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1

and let
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
satisfy (
4.24
).
Let
\tilde{X}_{0}\in C(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\tilde{X}_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
u\in C([0,T]\times\Omega;U)
.
If
(X,u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(\tilde{X}_{0})
and
\mu_{t}:=(X_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}
then there exists a unique
\tilde{X}\in C([0,T]\times\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfying for any
\omega\in\Omega

(4.25)

\begin{cases}\dot{\tilde{X}}_{t}(\omega)=f(\tilde{X}_{t}(\omega),u_{t}(\omega),\mu_{t}),&\forall\ t\in(0,T)\\
\tilde{X}_{|t=0}(\omega)=\tilde{X}_{0}(\omega).&\end{cases}

Moreover,
\tilde{X}_{t}(\omega)=X_{t}(\omega)
for every
t\in[0,T]
and
\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
.

Proof.

For any
\omega\in\Omega
, there exists a unique solution
\tilde{X}(\omega)\in C^{1}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})
of (
4.25
) thanks to the Lipschitz assumptions on the vector field
f
.
Since
u,(\mu_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
and
\tilde{X}_{0}
are fixed,
the solutions of (
4.1
) and (
4.25
) coincide, hence
\tilde{X}_{t}(\omega)=X_{t}(\omega)
for any
t\in[0,T]
, for
\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
.

Denoting with
\tilde{X}:[0,T]\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
the function

\tilde{X}(t,\omega)=\tilde{X}_{t}(\omega)
, we prove the continuity of
\tilde{X}
.
We fix
(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\Omega
, and a sequence

(t_{n},\omega_{n})\in[0,T]\times\Omega
converging to
(t,\omega)
as
n\to+\infty
.
By triangular inequality,

|\tilde{X}_{t_{n}}(\omega_{n})-\tilde{X}_{t}(\omega)|\leq|\tilde{X}_{t_{n}}(\omega_{n})-\tilde{X}_{t_{n}}(\omega)|+|\tilde{X}_{t_{n}}(\omega)-\tilde{X}_{t}(\omega)|.

The second term is estimated by

|\tilde{X}_{t_{n}}(\omega)-\tilde{X}_{t}(\omega)|\leq\int_{t}^{t_{n}}\left|f(\tilde{X}_{s}(\omega),u_{s}(\omega),\mu_{s})\right|{\mathrm{d}}s.

Concerning the first term,

\begin{split}|\tilde{X}_{t_{n}}(\omega_{n})-\tilde{X}_{t_{n}}(\omega)|&\leq|\tilde{X}_{0}(\omega_{n})-\tilde{X}_{0}(\omega)|\\
&+\int_{0}^{t_{n}}\left|f(\tilde{X}_{s}(\omega_{n}),u_{s}(\omega_{n}),\mu_{s})-f(\tilde{X}_{s}(\omega),u_{s}(\omega_{n}),\mu_{s})\right|{\mathrm{d}}s\\
&+\int_{0}^{t_{n}}\left|f(\tilde{X}_{s}(\omega),u_{s}(\omega_{n}),\mu_{s})-f(\tilde{X}_{s}(\omega),u_{s}(\omega),\mu_{s})\right|{\mathrm{d}}s\\
&\leq|\tilde{X}_{0}(\omega_{n})-\tilde{X}_{0}(\omega)|+L\int_{0}^{t_{n}}|\tilde{X}_{s}(\omega_{n})-\tilde{X}_{s}(\omega)|\,{\mathrm{d}}s\\
&+\int_{0}^{T}\left|f(\tilde{X}_{s}(\omega),u_{s}(\omega_{n}),\mu_{s})-f(\tilde{X}_{s}(\omega),u_{s}(\omega),\mu_{s})\right|{\mathrm{d}}s.\end{split}

By Gronwall lemma we have

\begin{split}|\tilde{X}_{t_{n}}(\omega_{n})-\tilde{X}_{t_{n}}(\omega)|\leq e^{LT}\left(|\tilde{X}_{0}(\omega_{n})-\tilde{X}_{0}(\omega)|+\int_{0}^{T}R_{s}(\omega_{n},\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}s\,\right),\end{split}

where

R_{s}(\omega_{n},\omega):=\left|f(\tilde{X}_{s}(\omega),u_{s}(\omega_{n}),\mu_{s})-f(\tilde{X}_{s}(\omega),u_{s}(\omega),\mu_{s})\right|
.
Collecting the previous inequalities we get

\begin{split}|\tilde{X}_{t_{n}}(\omega_{n})-\tilde{X}_{t}(\omega)|&\leq e^{LT}\left(|\tilde{X}_{0}(\omega_{n})-\tilde{X}_{0}(\omega)|+\int_{0}^{T}R_{s}(\omega_{n},\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}s\,\right)\\
&+\int_{t}^{t_{n}}\left|f(\tilde{X}_{s}(\omega),u_{s}(\omega),\mu_{s})\right|{\mathrm{d}}s,\end{split}

By the continuity of
\tilde{X}_{0}
, the growth property (
3.3
) and the continuity of
u

we can pass to the limit in the right hand side and we conclude.

∎

Proposition 4.21

(Stability for
\mathbb{P}
)
.

Let
\mathbb{S}:=(U,f,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1

and
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
satisfying (
4.24
).
Let
\mathbb{P}^{n},\mathbb{P}\in\mathscr{P}(\Omega)
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
\mathbb{P}^{n}\to\mathbb{P}
weakly.
Let
\tilde{X}_{0}\in C(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
u\in C([0,T]\times\Omega;U)
such that
\tilde{X}_{0}\in\left[\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}L^{p}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})\right]\cap L^{p}_{\mathbb{P}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})

and

(4.26)

\|\tilde{X}_{0}\|_{L^{p}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\to\|\tilde{X}_{0}\|_{L^{p}_{\mathbb{P}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\quad\text{ if }\ n\to+\infty.

We denote by
\textup{{L}}^{n}:=\textup{{L}}(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P}^{n})
and
\textup{{L}}:=\textup{{L}}(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
.

Let
(X,u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(\tilde{X}_{0})
and
(X^{n},u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}^{n}}(\tilde{X}_{0})
and denote with
\tilde{X},\tilde{X}^{n}\in C([0,T]\times\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
the corresponding solutions given in Lemma
4.20
associated with
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{P}^{n}
, respectively.
Then

(4.27)

\sup_{(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\Omega}|\tilde{X}^{n}_{t}(\omega)-\tilde{X}_{t}(\omega)|\to 0,\qquad\text{ as }n\to+\infty,

(4.28)

J_{\textup{{L}}^{n}}(X^{n},u)\longrightarrow J_{\textup{{L}}}(X,u),\qquad\text{ as }n\to+\infty.

Proof.

We denote
\mu^{n}_{t}:=(X^{n}_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}^{n}
and
\mu_{t}:=(X_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}
.

Since
\tilde{X}_{0}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is continuous, the weak convergence
\mathbb{P}^{n}\to\mathbb{P}
implies that

\mu_{0}^{n}=(\tilde{X}_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}^{n}\to\mu_{0}=(\tilde{X}_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}
weakly and (
4.26
) guarantees
\mathrm{m}_{p}(\mu_{0}^{n})\to\mathrm{m}_{p}(\mu_{0})
.
Consequently, by Proposition
2.4
, it holds
W_{p}(\mu_{0}^{n},\mu_{0})\to 0
as
n\to+\infty
and there exists an admissible
\psi:[0,+\infty)\to[0,+\infty)
,
according to Definition
2.3
, such that

(4.29)

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(|x|^{p})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{0}^{n}(x)<+\infty.

Using the same argument of the proof of Lemma
4.10
, thanks to the estimates (
4.11
)
and (
4.10
)
there exist
\tilde{\mu}\in C([0,T];\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and a (not relabelled) subsequence such that

(4.30)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\sup_{s\in[0,T]}W_{p}(\mu^{n}_{s},\tilde{\mu}_{s})=0.

We define
\bar{X}\in C([0,T]\times\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
through the system (
4.25
)
using
\tilde{\mu}_{t}
instead of
\mu_{t}
, i.e., for any
\omega\in\Omega
,
t\mapsto\bar{X}(t,\omega)
is the
solution of the problem

(4.31)

\begin{cases}\dot{\bar{X}}_{t}(\omega)=f(\bar{X}_{t}(\omega),u_{t}(\omega),\tilde{\mu}_{t}),&\forall\,t\in(0,T)\\
\bar{X}_{|t=0}(\omega)=\tilde{X}_{0}(\omega).&\end{cases}

We show that

(4.32)

\sup_{(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\Omega}|\tilde{X}^{n}_{t}(\omega)-\bar{X}_{t}(\omega)|\to 0,\qquad\text{ as }n\to+\infty.

Indeed, for any
\omega\in\Omega
and
t\in[0,T]
,

\begin{split}|\tilde{X}_{t}^{n}(\omega)-\bar{X}_{t}(\omega)|&\leq\int_{0}^{t}\left|f(\tilde{X}_{s}^{n}(\omega),u_{s}(\omega),\mu^{n}_{s})-f(\bar{X}_{s}(\omega),u_{s}(\omega),\tilde{\mu}_{s})\right|{\mathrm{d}}s\\
&\leq L\int_{0}^{t}\left(|\tilde{X}_{s}^{n}(\omega)-\bar{X}_{s}(\omega)|+W_{p}(\mu^{n}_{s},\tilde{\mu}_{s})\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}s,\end{split}

and, by Gronwall inequality, we obtain

|\tilde{X}_{t}^{n}(\omega)-\bar{X}_{t}(\omega)|\leq LTe^{LT}\sup_{s\in[0,T]}W_{p}(\mu^{n}_{s},\tilde{\mu}_{s}),

which, by (
4.30
), proves (
4.32
).

We have to show that
\bar{X}=\tilde{X}
.
We first prove that
\tilde{\mu}_{t}=(\tilde{X}_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}
.
By the uniform convergence (
4.32
), the continuity of
\bar{X}_{t}
and the weak convergence
\mathbb{P}^{n}\to\mathbb{P}
,
we obtain that (see
[
3
, Lemma 5.2.1]
)

\int_{\Omega}\phi(\tilde{X}_{t}^{n}(\omega))\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}^{n}(\omega)\to\int_{\Omega}\phi(\bar{X}_{t}(\omega))\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega),\qquad\forall\,\phi\in{C}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}).

Since
\displaystyle{\int_{\Omega}\phi(\tilde{X}_{t}^{n}(\omega))\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}^{n}(\omega)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\phi(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}^{n}(x)}
,
by the uniqueness of the weak limit we obtain that
\tilde{\mu}_{t}=(\bar{X}_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}
.
Then,
\bar{X}_{t}(\omega)
satisfies

(4.33)

\begin{cases}\dot{\bar{X}}_{t}(\omega)=f(\bar{X}_{t}(\omega),u_{t}(\omega),(\bar{X}_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}),&\forall\,t\in(0,T)\\
\bar{X}_{|t=0}(\omega)=\tilde{X}_{0}(\omega).&\end{cases}

By the uniqueness result of Proposition
4.8
and the definition of the Lagrangian problem, we obtain that
\bar{X}_{t}=X_{t}
in
L^{p}_{\mathbb{P}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
for any
t\in[0,T]
.
In particular we have that
\tilde{\mu}_{t}=\mu_{t}
for any
t\in[0,T]
.
It follows that the systems (
4.31
) and (
4.25
) are the same,
and then
\bar{X}_{t}(\omega)=\tilde{X}_{t}(\omega)
for any
(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\Omega
.
Finally, the convergence (
4.27
) follows by (
4.32
),
because the limit
\tilde{\mu}
given by the compactness is uniquely determined and it is independent of the subsequence.

For what concerns (
4.28
),
we first observe that

J_{\textup{{L}}^{n}}(X^{n},u)=J_{\textup{{L}}^{n}}(\tilde{X}^{n},u),\qquad J_{\textup{{L}}}(X,u)=J_{\textup{{L}}}(\tilde{X},u).

We write the running cost as

\begin{split}&\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{T}{\mathcal{C}}(\tilde{X}^{n}_{t}(\omega),u_{t}(\omega),\mu^{n}_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}^{n}(\omega)\\
&=\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{T}\left({\mathcal{C}}(\tilde{X}^{n}_{t}(\omega),u_{t}(\omega),\mu^{n}_{t})-{\mathcal{C}}(\tilde{X}_{t}(\omega),u_{t}(\omega),\mu_{t})\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}^{n}(\omega)\\
&+\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{T}{\mathcal{C}}(\tilde{X}_{t}(\omega),u_{t}(\omega),\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}^{n}(\omega).\end{split}

By (
4.27
) and the continuity of
{\mathcal{C}}
, we have that

\left({\mathcal{C}}(\tilde{X}^{n}_{t}(\omega),u_{t}(\omega),\mu^{n}_{t})-{\mathcal{C}}(\tilde{X}_{t}(\omega),u_{t}(\omega),\mu_{t})\right)\to 0

uniformly on compact sets of
[0,T]\times\Omega
.
By the weak convergence of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
towards
\mathbb{P}
we conclude that

\begin{split}\lim_{n\to+\infty}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{T}\left({\mathcal{C}}(\tilde{X}^{n}_{t}(\omega),u_{t}(\omega),\mu^{n}_{t})-{\mathcal{C}}(\tilde{X}_{t}(\omega),u_{t}(\omega),\mu_{t})\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}^{n}(\omega)=0.\end{split}

We have to prove that

(4.34)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{T}{\mathcal{C}}(\tilde{X}_{t}(\omega),u_{t}(\omega),\mu_{t}){\mathrm{d}}t\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}^{n}(\omega)=\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{T}{\mathcal{C}}(\tilde{X}_{t}(\omega),u_{t}(\omega),\mu_{t}){\mathrm{d}}t\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega).

By (
4.29
), which can be rewritten as

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{\Omega}\psi(|\tilde{X}_{0}(\omega)|^{p})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}^{n}(\omega)<+\infty,

by estimate (
4.11
) and the doubling property of
\psi
we get

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{\Omega}\psi(|\tilde{X}_{t}(\omega)|^{p})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}^{n}(\omega)<+\infty.

By the growth condition (
3.2
) and the doubling property of
\psi
we obtain that
the map
(t,\omega)\mapsto{\mathcal{C}}(\tilde{X}_{t}(\omega),u_{t}(\omega),\mu_{t})
is uniformly
integrable w.r.t.
\{{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\otimes\mathbb{P}^{n}\}_{n}
.
Since this map is also continuous, by
[
3
, Lemma 5.1.7]
we obtain (
4.34
).

Analogously one proves that

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{C}_{T}(\tilde{X}^{n}_{T}(\omega),\mu^{n}_{T})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}^{n}(\omega)=\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{C}_{T}(\tilde{X}_{T}(\omega),\mu_{T})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega).

∎

Proposition 4.22

(Approximation by continuous controls)
.

Let
\mathbb{S}:=(U,f,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1
with
U
a convex compact subset of a separable Banach space
V
, and
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
satisfying (
4.24
).
Let
\tilde{X}_{0}\in C(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
u\in\mathrm{M}([0,T]\times\Omega;U)
such that
\tilde{X}_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
If
(X,u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(\tilde{X}_{0})
then there exits a sequence
(\tilde{X}^{n},u^{n})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(\tilde{X}_{0})
such that

(1)

u^{n}\in C([0,T]\times\Omega;U)
and
\tilde{X}^{n}\in C([0,T]\times\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
;

(2)

u^{n}(t,\omega)\to u(t,\omega)
for
({\mathcal{L}}_{T}\otimes\mathbb{P})
-a.e.
(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\Omega
;

(3)

\,\lim_{n\to+\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\tilde{X}^{n}_{t}(\omega)-X_{t}(\omega)|=0
for
\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
,

\,\lim_{n\to+\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\tilde{X}^{n}_{t}-X_{t}\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}=0
;

(4)

J_{\textup{{L}}}(\tilde{X}^{n},u^{n})\to J_{\textup{{L}}}(X,u)
, as
n\to+\infty
.

Proof.

Since
u\in\mathrm{M}([0,T]\times\Omega;U)
,
by Lusin’s theorem applied to the space
[0,T]\times\Omega
with the measure
{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\otimes\mathbb{P}
,
there exists a sequence of compact subsets
A_{n}\subset[0,T]\times\Omega

such that
A_{n}\subset A_{n+1}
,
{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\otimes\mathbb{P}([0,T]\times\Omega\setminus A_{n})<\frac{1}{n}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

u|_{A_{n}}:A_{n}\to U
is continuous.
Applying Dugundji’s extension theorem
[
30
, Theorem 4.1]
we can extend
u|_{A_{n}}
to a continuous map

u^{n}:[0,T]\times\Omega\to V
such that
u^{n}([0,T]\times\Omega)
is contained in the closed convex subset
U
of
V
.
Moreover, for
({\mathcal{L}}_{T}\otimes\mathbb{P})
-a.e.
(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\Omega
it holds that
u^{n}(t,\omega)\to u(t,\omega)
,
thanks to the convergence
{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\otimes\mathbb{P}([0,T]\times\Omega\setminus A_{n})\to 0
as
n\to+\infty
.

Thanks to Proposition
4.8
and Lemma
4.20
, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a unique
\tilde{X}^{n}\in C([0,T]\times\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
(\tilde{X}^{n},u^{n})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(\tilde{X}_{0})
.
Defining
\mu^{n}_{t}:=(\tilde{X}^{n}_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}
, by Lemma
4.10
there exists
\bar{\mu}\in C([0,T];\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))

such that, up to subsequences,

(4.35)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}W_{p}(\mu^{n}_{t},\bar{\mu}_{t})\to 0,\qquad\text{ as }n\to+\infty.

For every
\omega\in\Omega
we define
\bar{X}(\omega)
as the unique solution to the problem

\begin{cases}\dot{\bar{X}}_{t}(\omega)=f(\bar{X}_{t}(\omega),u_{t}(\omega),\bar{\mu}_{t}),&\textrm{for a.e. }t\in(0,T)\\
\bar{X}_{|t=0}(\omega)=\tilde{X}_{0}(\omega).&\end{cases}

Then for any
(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\Omega
it holds

(4.36)

\begin{split}|\tilde{X}_{t}^{n}(\omega)-\bar{X}_{t}(\omega)|&\leq\int_{0}^{t}\left|f(\tilde{X}^{n}_{s}(\omega),u^{n}_{s}(\omega),\mu^{n}_{s})-f(\bar{X}_{s}(\omega),u^{n}_{s}(\omega),\bar{\mu}_{s})\right|{\mathrm{d}}s\\
&+\int_{0}^{t}\left|f(\bar{X}_{s}(\omega),u^{n}_{s}(\omega),\bar{\mu}_{s})-f(\bar{X}_{s}(\omega),u_{s}(\omega),\bar{\mu}_{s})\right|{\mathrm{d}}s\\
&\leq L\int_{0}^{t}\left(|\tilde{X}_{s}^{n}(\omega)-\bar{X}_{s}(\omega)|+W_{p}(\mu^{n}_{s},\bar{\mu}_{s})+\mathcal{G}_{s,\omega}(u^{n}_{s}(\omega),u_{s}(\omega))\right){\mathrm{d}}s,\end{split}

where
\mathcal{G}_{s,\omega}(u^{n}_{s}(\omega),u_{s}(\omega)):=\left|f(\bar{X}_{s}(\omega),u^{n}_{s}(\omega),\bar{\mu}_{s})-f(\bar{X}_{s}(\omega),u_{s}(\omega),\bar{\mu}_{s})\right|
.
Since by (
3.3
) we have

\mathcal{G}_{s,\omega}(u^{n}_{s}(\omega),u_{s}(\omega))\leq C(1+|\bar{X}_{s}(\omega)|+m_{p}(\bar{\mu}_{s}))
,
by the convergence in item
(2)
we get that

(4.37)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{G}_{s,\omega}(u^{n}_{s}(\omega),u_{s}(\omega)){\mathrm{d}}s=0,\quad\mbox{for $\mathbb{P}$-a.e. }\omega\in\Omega.

By Gronwall inequality, from (
4.36
) we have

(4.38)

\begin{split}|\tilde{X}_{t}^{n}(\omega)-\bar{X}_{t}(\omega)|&\leq e^{LT}LT\Big(\sup_{s\in[0,T]}W_{p}(\mu^{n}_{s},\bar{\mu}_{s})+\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{G}_{s,\omega}(u^{n}_{s}(\omega),u_{s}(\omega))\,{\mathrm{d}}s\Big),\end{split}

which, by (
4.35
) and (
4.37
), implies

(4.39)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\tilde{X}^{n}_{t}(\omega)-\bar{X}_{t}(\omega)|=0,\quad\mbox{for $\mathbb{P}$-a.e. }\omega\in\Omega.

From (
4.39
) we have that
\mu_{t}^{n}
weakly converges to
(\bar{X}_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}
and by (
4.35
) it follows that

\bar{\mu}_{t}=(\bar{X}_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}
for any
t\in[0,T]
.
By the definition of
\bar{X}
and
\bar{\mu}_{t}=(\bar{X}_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}
and by
the uniqueness result of Proposition
4.8
, we obtain that

\bar{X}_{t}(\omega)=X_{t}(\omega)
for
\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
and
for any
t\in[0,T]
.
The first convergence in item
(3)
follows from (
4.39
), while the second convergence comes from the first one and
Proposition
4.9
through dominated convergence.

Finally, item
(4)
follows by the same argument as in the proof of Proposition
4.11
.
∎

5.
Relaxed Lagrangian optimal control problem

In this Section we define a relaxed version of the Lagrangian problem analyzed in Section
4
, then we study its properties and its relation with the non-relaxed one.

Definition 5.1

(Relaxed Lagrangian optimal control problem (
RL
))
.

Let
\mathbb{S}:=(U,f,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1

and let
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space.
Given
X_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we say that
(X,\sigma)\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{RL}}}(X_{0})

if

(i)

\sigma\in\mathrm{M}([0,T]\times\Omega;\mathscr{P}(U))
;

(ii)

X\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathrm{AC}^{p}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))

and for
\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
,
X(\omega)

is a solution of the following Cauchy problem

(5.1)

\begin{cases}\dot{X}_{t}(\omega)=\displaystyle\int_{U}f(X_{t}(\omega),u,(X_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P})\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma_{t,\omega}(u),&\textrm{for ${\mathcal{L}}_{T}$-a.e. }t\in]0,T]\\
X_{|t=0}(\omega)=X_{0}(\omega),&\end{cases}

where
X_{t}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is defined by
X_{t}(\omega):=X(t,\omega)
for
\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
and

\sigma_{t,\omega}:=\sigma(t,\omega)\in\mathscr{P}(U)
.

We refer to
(X,u)\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{RL}}}(X_{0})
as to an
admissible pair
, with
X
a
trajectory
and
\sigma
a
relaxed control
.

We define the
cost functional

J_{\textup{{RL}}}:L^{p}(\Omega;C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times\mathrm{M}([0,T]\times\Omega;\mathscr{P}(U))\to[0,+\infty)
, by

J_{\textup{{RL}}}(X,\sigma):=\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}\mathcal{C}(X_{t}(\omega),u,(X_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P})\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma_{t,\omega}(u)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)\ +\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{C}_{T}(X_{T}(\omega),(X_{T})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega),

and the
value function

V_{\textup{{RL}}}:L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})\to[0,+\infty)
by

(5.2)

V_{\textup{{RL}}}(X_{0}):=\inf\left\{J_{\textup{{RL}}}(X,\sigma)\,:\,(X,\sigma)\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{RL}}}(X_{0})\right\}.

Remark 5.2
.

By Proposition
3.6
the Relaxed Lagrangian problem
RL
in
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
is a particular
Lagrangian convex problem
\textup{{L}}^{\prime}
in the lifted space
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}=(\mathscr{U},\mathscr{F},\mathscr{C},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
defined in Definition
3.5
.
In particular, the system (
5.1
) can be rewritten as

\begin{cases}\dot{X}_{t}(\omega)=\mathscr{F}(X_{t}(\omega),\sigma_{t}(\omega),(X_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}),&\textrm{for a.e. }t\in(0,T]\\
X_{|t=0}(\omega)=X_{0}(\omega).&\end{cases}

and the cost functional as

J_{\textup{{RL}}}(X,\sigma):=\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{T}\mathscr{C}(X_{t}(\omega),\sigma(t,\omega),(X_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P})\,{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)+\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{C}_{T}(X_{T}(\omega),(X_{T})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega).

As a consequence, the results proved for the Lagrangian problem
L
also apply to the Relaxed Lagrangian problem
RL
.
We further point out that even in the relaxed Lagrangian setting, existence of minimizers is not guaranteed in general (see also Remark
4.2
).
We refer to Section
8.3
for a detailed discussion and in particular to Remark
8.10
.

5.1.
Equivalence of
L
and
RL
. Chattering result

In this subsection we prove that the value functions for the Lagrangian and the Relaxed Lagrangian optimal control problems, set in the same parametrization space
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
and same system
\mathbb{S}
, coincide.
Precisely, we aim at showing the following theorem whose proof is postponed at the end of the section.

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
\mathbb{S}:=(U,f,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1
and
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space
such that there exists
\{\mathfrak{B}^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

satisfying the finite approximation property of Definition
4.15
.
If
X_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})=V_{\textup{{RL}}}(X_{0})
.

The proof of Theorem
5.3
easily follows from the combination of Theorem
5.6
and Proposition
5.4
given below. Theorem
5.6
is a suitable extension of the classical (in optimal control theory)
chattering theorem
which permits to approximate relaxed controls with piecewise-constant controls.

Notice that Theorem
5.3
holds in particular if
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
is a standard Borel space thanks to Proposition
4.16
.

Let us start with the following proposition.

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
\mathbb{S}:=(U,f,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1
and

(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space.
Let
X_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
. If
(X,u)\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})
, then,
defining
\sigma:[0,T]\times\Omega\to\mathscr{P}(U)
by
\sigma(t,\omega)=\delta_{u(t,\omega)}
we have

(X,\sigma)\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{RL}}}(X_{0})
and
J_{\textup{{L}}}(X,u)=J_{\textup{{RL}}}(X,\sigma)
.
In particular
V_{\textup{{RL}}}(X_{0})\leq V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})
.

Proof.

The result follows immediately by Proposition
3.6
.
∎

Recall that if
\bar{\mathfrak{B}}
is a finite algebra on
\Omega
and
E
is a Banach space, a function
g:\Omega\to E
is

\bar{\mathfrak{B}}
-measurable if and only if
g
is constant on the elements of a partition of
\Omega
contained in
\bar{\mathfrak{B}}
.

In the following proposition, given a piecewise constant
relaxed
control we approximate it with a sequence of piecewise constant
(non-relaxed)
controls so that the associated trajectories and costs converge.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
\mathbb{S}:=(U,f,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1
and

(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space.
Let
X_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\bar{\mathfrak{B}}\subset\mathfrak{B}
a finite algebra,

(X,\sigma)\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{RL}}}(X_{0})
such that

\sigma
is
({\mathcal{B}}_{[0,T]}\otimes\bar{\mathfrak{B}})
-measurable.
Then there exists a sequence
\{(X^{m},u^{m})\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\subset{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})
such that

(1)

u^{m}
are
({\mathcal{B}}_{[0,T]}\otimes\bar{\mathfrak{B}})
-measurable;

(2)

for any
\omega\in\Omega
,

(i_{[0,T]},u^{m}(\cdot,\omega))_{\sharp}{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}}\sigma_{\omega}
,
where
\sigma_{\omega}:=\sigma(t,\omega)\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\in\mathscr{P}([0,T]\times U)
;

(3)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|X^{m}_{t}-X_{t}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\to 0
as
m\to+\infty
;

(4)

J_{\textup{{L}}}(X^{m},u^{m})\to J_{\textup{{RL}}}(X,\sigma)
, as
m\to+\infty
.

Moreover, if
X_{0}
is
\bar{\mathfrak{B}}
-measurable then

X_{t}
,
X_{t}^{m}
are
\bar{\mathfrak{B}}
-measurable for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in[0,T]
.

Proof.

We fix the minimal (w.r.t. inclusion) partition associated to the finite algebra
\bar{\mathfrak{B}}
that we denote by
{\mathcal{P}}:=\{A_{k}:k=1,\ldots n\}\subset\bar{\mathfrak{B}}
.
For any
k=1,\ldots n
we select
\omega_{k}\in A_{k}
and apply
Lemma
2.10
(with
\mathbb{T}=[0,T]
,
S=U
and
\lambda={\mathcal{L}}_{T}
) to the measure

\nu=\sigma_{\omega_{k}}:=\sigma(t,\omega_{k})\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\in\mathscr{P}([0,T]\times U)
.
This yields a sequence of
{\mathcal{B}}_{[0,T]}
-measurable functions
u^{m}_{k}:[0,T]\to U
such that

(i_{[0,T]},u^{m}_{k}(\cdot))_{\sharp}{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}}\sigma_{\omega_{k}}.

Thus, we define
u^{m}:[0,T]\times\Omega\to U
setting
u^{m}(t,\omega):=u^{m}_{k}(t)
if
\omega\in A_{k}
.
By construction, the function
\omega\mapsto u^{m}(t,\omega)
is constant
on
A_{k}
, for any
k=1,\ldots,n
.
Furthermore, for any
\omega\in\Omega
the maps
t\mapsto u^{m}(t,\omega)
are
{\mathcal{B}}_{[0,T]}
-measurable.
The sequence of controls
u^{m}\in\mathrm{M}([0,T]\times\Omega)
readily satisfies items (
1
) and (
2
).

Given
u^{m}
constructed above, by Proposition
4.8
there exists a unique
X^{m}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathrm{AC}^{p}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))
such that
(X^{m},u^{m})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})
.
Thanks to Remark,
4.3
we interpret
X^{m}\in\mathrm{AC}^{p}([0,T];L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and define
\mu^{m}\in\mathrm{AC}^{p}([0,T];\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
by
\mu_{t}^{m}:=(X_{t}^{m})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}
.
By Lemma
4.10

there exists a (non relabeled) subsequence
\mu^{m}
and

\tilde{\mu}\in C([0,T];\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
such that

(5.3)

\lim_{m\to\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}W_{p}(\mu^{m}_{t},\tilde{\mu}_{t})=0.

We define
g:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

g(t,y,u):=f(y,u,\tilde{\mu}_{t}).

Selecting a representative
X_{0}
defined for every
\omega\in\Omega
, let
Y^{m}(\omega)\in\mathrm{AC}^{p}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})
be the unique solution of the Cauchy problem

(5.4)

\begin{cases}\dot{Y}^{m}_{t}(\omega)=g(t,Y^{m}_{t}(\omega),u^{m}(t,\omega)),&\textrm{for a.e. }t\in(0,T)\\
Y^{m}_{|t=0}(\omega)=X_{0}(\omega).\end{cases}

For any
\omega\in\Omega
, let also

Y(\omega)\in\mathrm{AC}^{p}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})
be the unique solution of the Cauchy problem

(5.5)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\dot{Y}_{t}(\omega)=\int_{U}g(t,Y_{t}(\omega),u)\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma_{t,\omega}(u),&\textrm{for a.e. }t\in(0,T)\\
Y_{|t=0}(\omega)=X_{0}(\omega).\end{cases}

Hence, by item
(2)
and assumptions (
3.1
) and (
3.3
) we can apply Lemma
B.1
to obtain

(5.6)

\lim_{m\to+\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|Y^{m}_{t}(\omega)-Y_{t}(\omega)\right|=0,\qquad\forall\,\omega\in\Omega.

Since
(X^{m},u^{m})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})
, by definition of the Lagrangian problem, for
\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega

X^{m}(\omega)\in\mathrm{AC}^{p}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})
and

(5.7)

\begin{cases}\dot{X}^{m}_{t}(\omega)=f(X^{m}_{t}(\omega),u^{m}_{t}(\omega),\mu^{m}_{t}),\quad\textrm{for a.e. }t\in(0,T)&\\
X^{m}_{|t=0}(\omega)=X_{0}(\omega).\end{cases}

Then

\begin{split}\left|Y^{m}_{t}(\omega)-X^{m}_{t}(\omega)\right|&\leq\int_{0}^{t}|f(Y^{m}_{s}(\omega),u^{m}(s,\omega),\tilde{\mu}_{s})-f(X^{m}_{s}(\omega),u^{m}(s,\omega),\mu^{m}_{s})|\,{\mathrm{d}}s\\
&\leq L\int_{0}^{t}\left(\left|Y^{m}_{s}(\omega)-X^{m}_{s}(\omega)\right|+W_{p}(\tilde{\mu}_{s},\mu_{s}^{m})\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}s\\
&\leq L\int_{0}^{t}\left|Y^{m}_{s}(\omega)-X^{m}_{s}(\omega)\right|\,{\mathrm{d}}s+LT\sup_{s\in[0,T]}W_{p}(\tilde{\mu}_{s},\mu_{s}^{m}).\end{split}

By Gronwall inequality we get

(5.8)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|Y^{m}_{t}(\omega)-X^{m}_{t}(\omega)\right|\leq LTe^{LT}\sup_{s\in[0,T]}W_{p}(\tilde{\mu}_{s},\mu_{s}^{m}).

From (
5.8
), (
5.3
) and (
5.6
) it follows that

(5.9)

\lim_{m\to+\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|X^{m}_{t}(\omega)-Y_{t}(\omega)\right|=0,\qquad\text{for }\mathbb{P}\text{-a.e. }\omega\in\Omega.

By (
5.9
) it follows that
\mu^{m}_{t}=(X^{m}_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\to(Y_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}
weakly for any
t\in[0,T]
,
and then, by (
5.3
), it holds that
\tilde{\mu}_{t}=(Y_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}
for any
t\in[0,T]
.
Thus, thanks to (
5.5
) and the definition of
g
we conclude that
(Y,\sigma)\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{RL}}}(X_{0})
.
Since
(X,\sigma)\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{RL}}}(X_{0})
, by the uniqueness result of Propositions
4.8
we have that
Y=X

and

(5.10)

\lim_{m\to+\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|X^{m}_{t}(\omega)-X_{t}(\omega)\right|=0,\qquad\text{for }\mathbb{P}\text{-a.e. }\omega\in\Omega.

Finally, to prove item (3) it is enough to observe that

(5.11)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left\|X^{m}_{t}-X_{t}\right\|^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\int_{\Omega}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left|X^{m}_{t}(\omega)-X_{t}(\omega)\right|^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega).

By (
5.9
), and (
4.11
) we can pass to the limit in (
5.11
) by dominated convergence.

To prove item (
4
) we write

(5.12)

\begin{split}&\left|\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{T}{\mathcal{C}}(X^{m}_{t}(\omega),u^{m}_{t}(\omega),\mu^{m}_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)-\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{U}{\mathcal{C}}(X_{t}(\omega),u,\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma_{t,\omega}(u)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)\right|\\
&\leq\left|\int_{\Omega}\left(\int_{0}^{T}{\mathcal{C}}(X^{m}_{t}(\omega),u^{m}_{t}(\omega),\mu^{m}_{t})-{\mathcal{C}}(X_{t}(\omega),u^{m}_{t}(\omega),\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}t\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)\right|\\
&+\left|\int_{\Omega}\left(\int_{0}^{T}{\mathcal{C}}(X_{t}(\omega),u^{m}_{t}(\omega),\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}t-\int_{[0,T]\times U}{\mathcal{C}}(X_{t}(\omega),u,\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma_{\omega}(t,u)\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)\right|.\\
\end{split}

Since (
5.10
) holds, for
\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
, there exists a compact
K_{\omega}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that

X^{m}_{t}(\omega),X_{t}(\omega)\in K_{\omega}
for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in[0,T]
.
Analogously, by (
5.3
) there exists a compact
{\mathcal{K}}\subset\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

\mu^{m}_{t},\mu_{t}\in{\mathcal{K}}
for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in[0,T]
.
By Proposition
2.4
there exists an admissible
\psi
such that

(5.13)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sup_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{\Omega}\psi(|X^{m}_{t}(\omega)|^{p})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sup_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(|x|^{p})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}^{m}(x)<+\infty.

By the continuity of
{\mathcal{C}}
there exists a modulus of continuity
\alpha_{\omega}:[0,+\infty)\to[0,+\infty)

for the restriction of
{\mathcal{C}}
to the compact set
K_{\omega}\times U\times{\mathcal{K}}
.
Then, for
\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
,

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|{\mathcal{C}}(X_{t}^{m}(\omega),u_{t}^{m}(\omega),\mu_{t}^{m})-{\mathcal{C}}(X_{t}(\omega),u_{t}^{m}(\omega),\mu_{t})|\leq\alpha_{\omega}\big(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}(|X_{t}^{m}(\omega)-X_{t}(\omega)|+W_{p}(\mu_{t}^{m},\mu_{t}))\big).

Taking into account the previous consideration together with (
5.10
), (
5.3
),
the growth condition (
3.2
) and (
5.13
),
we obtain

\left|\int_{\Omega}\left(\int_{0}^{T}{\mathcal{C}}(X^{m}_{t}(\omega),u^{m}_{t}(\omega),\mu^{m}_{t})-{\mathcal{C}}(X_{t}(\omega),u^{m}_{t}(\omega),\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}t\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)\right|\to 0.

For the second term in the right hand side of (
5.12
), for
\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
we define
h_{\omega}:[0,T]\times U\to\mathbb{R}
by

h_{\omega}(t,u):={\mathcal{C}}(X_{t}(\omega),u,(X_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P})
.
Notice that
h_{\omega}
is continuous and bounded in
[0,T]\times U
, hence from the Young convergence of item (2) we get

(5.14)

\left|\int_{0}^{T}h_{\omega}(t,u^{m}_{t}(\omega))\,{\mathrm{d}}t-\int_{[0,T]\times U}h_{\omega}(t,u)\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma_{\omega}(t,u)\right|\to 0,\qquad\text{ for $\mathbb{P}$-a.e. }\omega\in\Omega.

From the growth assumptions (
3.2
) and dominated convergence theorem we obtain that

\left|\int_{\Omega}\left(\int_{0}^{T}{\mathcal{C}}(X_{t}(\omega),u^{m}_{t}(\omega),\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}t-\int_{[0,T]\times U}{\mathcal{C}}(X_{t}(\omega),u,\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma_{\omega}(t,u)\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)\right|\to 0.

Finally, thanks to (
5.10
) and (
5.3
) we also obtain that

\lim_{m\to+\infty}\int_{\Omega}{\mathcal{C}}_{T}(X^{m}_{T}(\omega),\mu^{m}_{T})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)=\int_{\Omega}{\mathcal{C}}_{T}(X_{T}(\omega),\mu_{T})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega).

For what concerns the last statement, since
X_{0}
is
\bar{\mathfrak{B}}
-measurable, (hence constant on the elements of the partition
{\mathcal{P}}
), the
measurability of
X_{t}^{m}
with respect to the algebra
\bar{\mathfrak{B}}
follows by uniqueness of solutions to (
5.7
).
The same argument also yields that
X_{t}
is
\bar{\mathfrak{B}}
-measurable.

∎

Combining Theorem
4.17
and Proposition
4.11
applied to the Relaxed Lagrangian problem
RL
, with
Proposition
5.5
, we can prove the following Theorem.

Theorem 5.6

(Chattering)
.

Let
\mathbb{S}:=(U,f,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1
.
Let
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space and
\{\mathfrak{B}^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

satisfying the finite approximation property of Definition
4.15
.
Let
X_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
(X,\sigma)\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{RL}}}(X_{0})
and

\{X^{n}_{0}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
, such that

(5.15)

\|X^{n}_{0}-X_{0}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\to 0\quad\text{ as }n\to+\infty.

Then there exists a sequence
\{(\tilde{X}^{n},\tilde{u}^{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
(\tilde{X}^{n},\tilde{u}^{n})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X^{n}_{0})
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and the following hold

(1)

\tilde{u}^{n}
are
({\mathcal{B}}_{[0,T]}\otimes\mathfrak{B}^{n})
-measurable;

(2)

for
\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
,
(i_{[0,T]},\tilde{u}^{n}(\cdot,\omega))_{\sharp}{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}}\sigma_{\omega}
,
as
n\to+\infty
,
where
\sigma_{\omega}:=\sigma(t,\omega)\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\in\mathscr{P}([0,T]\times U)
;

(3)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\tilde{X}^{n}_{t}-X_{t}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\to 0
as
n\to+\infty
;

(4)

J_{\textup{{L}}}(\tilde{X}^{n},\tilde{u}^{n})\to J_{\textup{{RL}}}(X,\sigma)
, as
n\to+\infty
.

Moreover, if
X_{0}^{n}
is
\mathfrak{B}^{n}
-measurable,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then

\tilde{X}_{t}^{n}
is
\mathfrak{B}^{n}
-measurable for any
t\in[0,T]
.

Proof.

Let
(X,\sigma)\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{RL}}}(X_{0})
and
\{X^{n}_{0}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfying (
5.15
).
Applying Theorem
4.17
to the relaxed problem
RL
(which is a Lagrangian problem in a lifted space as discussed in Remark
5.2
),
there exists a sequence
\{(X^{n},\sigma^{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
(X^{n},\sigma^{n})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{RL}}}(X^{n}_{0})
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Moreover
\sigma^{n}
are
({\mathcal{B}}_{[0,T]}\otimes\mathfrak{B}^{n})
-measurable,
\sigma^{n}\to\sigma
in
({\mathcal{L}}_{T}\otimes\mathbb{P})
-measure and, as a consequence, we have that
\sigma^{n}(t,\omega)\to\sigma(t,\omega)
weakly in
\mathscr{P}(U)
for
{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\otimes\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
(t,\omega)
, up to a non-relabelled subsequence. Thus, by Remark
2.9
, we get (up to a non-relabelled subsequence)

(5.16)

\sigma^{n}_{\omega}:=\sigma^{n}(t,\omega)\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}}\sigma_{\omega},\qquad\text{ for $\mathbb{P}$-a.e. }\omega\in\Omega.

By Proposition
5.5
, for any fixed
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a sequence

\{(Y^{n,m},u^{n,m})\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\subset{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X^{n}_{0})
,
with
u^{n,m}

({\mathcal{B}}_{[0,T]}\otimes\mathfrak{B}^{n})
-measurable, such that

(i)

for any
\omega\in\Omega
,
(i_{[0,T]},u^{n,m}(\cdot,\omega))_{\sharp}{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}}\sigma^{n}_{\omega}
,
as
m\to+\infty
;

(ii)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|X^{n}_{t}-Y^{n,m}_{t}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\to 0
, as
m\to+\infty
;

(iii)

J_{\textup{{L}}}(Y^{n,m},u^{n,m})\to J_{\textup{{RL}}}(X^{n},\sigma^{n})
, as
m\to+\infty
.

Let us denote by
{\mathcal{P}}(\mathfrak{B}^{n}):=\{A^{n}_{k},k=1,\ldots,k(n)\}
the minimal (finite) partition induced by
\mathfrak{B}^{n}
.
Let also
\sigma_{\omega}^{n,m}:=(i_{[0,T]},u^{n,m}(\cdot,\omega))_{\sharp}{\mathcal{L}}_{T}
and observe that the map
\omega\mapsto\sigma_{\omega}^{n,m}
is constant on the elements of
{\mathcal{P}}^{n}
.
Then, if we select a representative
\omega_{k}\in A^{n}_{k}
for any
k=1,\ldots,k(n)
, from item (i) it follows that

(5.17)

\lim_{m\to+\infty}\sup_{k=1,\ldots,k(n)}\,\delta(\sigma^{n,m}_{\omega_{k}},\sigma^{n}_{\omega_{k}})=\lim_{m\to+\infty}\sup_{\omega\in\Omega}\,\delta(\sigma^{n,m}_{\omega},\sigma^{n}_{\omega})=0,

where
\delta
metrizes the Young convergence in
[0,T]\times U
.
Recall that Young convergence is indeed equivalent to the weak convergence in
[0,T]\times U
, see Remark
2.9
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
m(n)
be such that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|X^{n}_{t}-Y^{n,m(n)}_{t}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\frac{1}{n},

|J_{\textup{{L}}}(Y^{n,m(n)},u^{n,m(n)})-J_{\textup{{RL}}}(X^{n},\sigma^{n})|<\frac{1}{n}

and

\sup_{\omega\in\Omega}\,\delta(\sigma^{n,m(n)}_{\omega},\sigma^{n}_{\omega})<\frac{1}{n}.

Let us define
\tilde{X}^{n}:=Y^{n,m(n)}
, the control function
\tilde{u}^{n}:=u^{n,m(n)}:[0,T]\times\Omega\to U
and

\tilde{\sigma}_{\omega}^{n}:=\sigma_{\omega}^{n,m(n)}=(i_{[0,T]},\tilde{u}^{n}(\cdot,\omega))_{\sharp}{\mathcal{L}}_{T}
.
Notice that, by construction,
(\tilde{X}^{n},\tilde{u}^{n})\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{L}}}(X^{n}_{0})
.

Fix now
\varepsilon>0
.
By Theorem
4.17
there exists
n_{\varepsilon}
such that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|X^{n}_{t}-X_{t}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\varepsilon,\quad\forall n>n_{\varepsilon},

and

|J_{\textup{{RL}}}(X^{n},\sigma^{n})-J_{\textup{{RL}}}(X,\sigma)|<\varepsilon,\quad\forall n>n_{\varepsilon}.

Then, using the definition of
\tilde{X}^{n}
and
\tilde{u}^{n}
, for any
n>n_{\varepsilon}
it holds

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\tilde{X}^{n}_{t}-X_{t}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|Y^{n,m(n)}_{t}-X^{n}_{t}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|X^{n}_{t}-X_{t}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\frac{1}{n}+\varepsilon,

and

\displaystyle|J_{\textup{{L}}}(\tilde{X}^{n},\tilde{u}^{n})-J_{\textup{{RL}}}(X,\sigma)|

\displaystyle\leq|J_{\textup{{L}}}(Y^{n,m(n)},u^{n,m(n)})-J_{\textup{{RL}}}(X^{n},\sigma^{n})|

\displaystyle\quad+|J_{\textup{{RL}}}(X^{n},\sigma^{n})-J_{\textup{{RL}}}(X,\sigma)|<\frac{1}{n}+\varepsilon.

If we send
n\to+\infty
, items (3) and (4) follow by the arbitrariness of
\varepsilon>0
.

It remains to show item (2). Fix again
\varepsilon>0
and choose
\omega\in\Omega
for which the convergence in (
5.16
) holds.
Then, there exists
n_{\varepsilon}(\omega)>0
such that

\delta(\sigma^{n}_{\omega},\sigma_{\omega})<\varepsilon,\quad\forall n>n_{\varepsilon}(\omega),

and

\delta(\tilde{\sigma}^{n}_{\omega},\sigma_{\omega})\leq\delta(\tilde{\sigma}^{n}_{\omega},\sigma^{n}_{\omega})+\delta(\sigma^{n}_{\omega},\sigma_{\omega})<\frac{1}{n}+\varepsilon,\quad\forall\,n>n_{\varepsilon}(\omega).

Sending
n\to+\infty
we get item (2).
∎

6.
Eulerian optimal control problem

In this Section we describe the Eulerian formulation of the optimal control problem and
we study its properties under the Convexity Assumption
3.4
. In particular, as stated in Theorem
6.8
, in this setting we get the existence of minimizers.
Recall that
\mathrm{B}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d};U)
denotes the set of Borel measurable functions.

Definition 6.1

(Eulerian optimal control problem (
E
))
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1
.
Given
\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we say that

(\mu,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})
if

(i)

\underline{u}\in\mathrm{B}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d};U)
;

(ii)

\mu\in\mathrm{AC}^{p}([0,T];\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is a distributional solution of the Cauchy problem

(6.1)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\mathrm{div}\,(v_{t}\mu_{t})=0,&\textrm{in }[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\\
\mu_{t=0}=\mu_{0},&\end{cases}

where

v\in\mathrm{B}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
is defined by

v_{t}(x):=f(x,\underline{u}(t,x),\mu_{t})
and
\mu_{t}:=\mu(t)
.

We refer to
(\mu,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})
as to an
admissible pair
, with
\mu
a
measure trajectory
and
\underline{u}
a
Eulerian control
.

We define the
cost functional

J_{\textup{{E}}}:\operatorname{C}([0,T];\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times{\mathrm{B}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d};U)}\to[0,+\infty)
by

J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu,\underline{u}):=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{C}}(x,\underline{u}(t,x),\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}t+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{C}}_{T}(x,\mu_{T})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{T}(x),

and the
value function

V_{\textup{{E}}}:\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to[0,+\infty)
by

V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0}):=\inf\{J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu,\underline{u})\,:\,(\mu,\underline{u})\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})\}.

Remark 6.2
.

Notice that, given
\underline{u}\in\mathrm{B}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d};U)
,
\mu\in C([0,T];\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and setting
v_{t}(x):=f(x,\underline{u}(t,x),\mu_{t})
as in Definition
6.1
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v_{t}(x)|^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f(x,\underline{u}(t,x),\mu_{t})|^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{C}\left(1+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)\right)\leq\bar{C},\quad\forall t\in[0,T]

for some constants
\tilde{C},\bar{C}>0
, thanks to the growth condition (
3.3
) and since
\mu\in C([0,T];\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. In particular, we get
v\in L^{p}(0,T;L^{p}_{\mu_{t}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Thus, if
\mu
is also a distributional solution of (
6.1
), then
\mu\in\mathrm{AC}^{p}([0,T];\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Hence, in Definition
6.1
(ii) we could have just required
\mu\in C([0,T];\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.

Observe also that the functional
J_{\textup{{E}}}
is finite thanks to the growth condition (
3.2
).

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then
{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})\not=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Let us fix
u_{0}\in U
and define
\underline{u}(t,x)=u_{0}
for any
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Applying Proposition
4.8
with

\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathbb{P}=\mu_{0}
,
X_{0}(x)=x
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
u=\underline{u}
, there exists
X\in L^{p}_{\mu_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathrm{AC}^{p}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))
such that
(X,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})
.
Defining
\mu_{t}:=(X_{t})_{\sharp}\mu_{0}
, from the definition of the Lagrangian problem it holds

(6.2)

X_{t}(x)=x+\int_{0}^{t}f(X_{s}(x),u_{0},\mu_{s})\,{\mathrm{d}}s,\qquad\forall\,t\in[0,T]\text{ and }\mu_{0}\text{-a.e. }x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

Furthermore, in view of Proposition
A.3
we have that

X\in\mathrm{AC}^{p}([0,T];L^{p}_{\mu_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
from (
2.5
) we get
W_{p}(\mu_{t},\mu_{s})\leq\|X_{t}-X_{s}\|_{L^{p}_{\mu_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})}
for any
t,s\in[0,T]
.
This readily implies that
\mu\in\mathrm{AC}^{p}([0,T];\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.
If we define
v_{t}(x):=f(x,u_{0},\mu_{t})
, it remains to show that
\mu
is a distributional solution of (
6.1
).
This is a standard argument, in view of the fact that (
6.2
) represents the system of characteristics of (
6.1
) (see e.g.
[
3
, Lemma 8.1.6]
).
∎

Remark 6.4
.

Given
\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, general results granting the existence of solutions to the Cauchy problem (
6.1
) are provided for instance in
[
46
, Theorem A.2]
when
\underline{u}\in\mathrm{B}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d};U)
is also a Carathéodory function.

Definition 6.5
.

Let
U
be a subset of a separable Banach space
V
, and denote with
V^{\prime}
the dual of
V
. Let
(\mu^{n},\underline{u}^{n}),(\mu,\underline{u})\in\operatorname{C}([0,T];\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times{\mathrm{B}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d};U)}
.
We say that
(\mu^{n},\underline{u}^{n})
converges to
(\mu,\underline{u})
if

•

\mu^{n}
converges to
\mu
in
\operatorname{C}([0,T];\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,

•

for any
\phi\in C_{c}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d};V^{\prime})
we have

(6.3)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\langle\phi(t,x),\underline{u}^{n}(t,x)\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}^{n}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}t=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\langle\phi(t,x),\underline{u}(t,x)\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}t.

Proposition 6.6

(Compactness)
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfy the Convexity Assumption
3.4
.
Let
\mu_{0},\mu_{0}^{n}\in\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
W_{p}(\mu_{0}^{n},\mu_{0})\to 0
, as
n\to+\infty
.
If
(\mu^{n},\underline{u}^{n})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu^{n}_{0})
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then there exist
(\mu,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})

and a subsequence
(\mu^{n_{k}},\underline{u}^{n_{k}})
such that

(\mu^{n_{k}},\underline{u}^{n_{k}})
converges to
(\mu,\underline{u})
, as
k\to+\infty
, according to Definition
6.5
.

Proof.

Let
(\mu^{n},\underline{u}^{n})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu^{n}_{0})
.
Since
W_{p}(\mu_{0}^{n},\mu_{0})\to 0
, by Proposition
2.4
it holds that

(6.4)

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{p}{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{0}(x)<+\infty

and there exists an admissible
\psi:[0,+\infty)\to[0,+\infty)
, in the sense of Definition
2.3
, such that

(6.5)

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(|x|^{p}){\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{0}(x)<+\infty.

In order to apply Ascoli-Arzelà Theorem to the sequence
\{\mu^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, we show that

(6.6)

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(|x|^{p}){\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{t}(x)<+\infty

and there exists a constant
C
such that

(6.7)

W^{p}_{p}(\mu^{n}_{s},\mu^{n}_{t})\leq C|t-s|,\qquad\forall\,s,t\in[0,T].

We start by estimating
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{p}{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{t}(x)
.
We formally use the map
x\mapsto|x|^{p}
as a test function for the weak formulation of the continuity equation
(a rigorous approach would require an approximation of this map through cut-off functions,
see
[
34
, Section 5]
).
Defining
v^{n}(t,x):=f(x,\underline{u}^{n}(t,x),\mu^{n}_{t})
, using the growth condition on
f
given in (
3.3
) and Young inequality
we obtain that

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{t}(x)&\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{0}(x)+p\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v^{n}(s,x)||x|^{p-1}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{s}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}s\\
&\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{0}(x)+Cp\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(1+|x|+\mathrm{m}_{p}(\mu_{s}^{n})\right)|x|^{p-1}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{s}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}s\\
&\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{p}{\mathrm{d}}\,\mu^{n}_{0}(x)+\tilde{C}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{s}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}s,\end{split}

for some
\tilde{C}>0
independent of
n
and

t\in[0,T]
.
By Gronwall’s inequality and (
6.4
) we get that

(6.8)

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{p}{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{t}(x)<+\infty.

Formally using the map
x\mapsto\psi(|x|^{p})
as a test function for the weak formulation of the continuity equation,
by the growth condition on
f
in (
3.3
) and the bound (
6.8
),
we have

(6.9)

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(|x|^{p})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{t}(x)&\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(|x|^{p})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{0}(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v^{n}(s,x)||\nabla(\psi(|x|^{p}))|\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{s}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}s\\
&\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(|x|^{p})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{0}(x)+pC\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(1+|x|+\mathrm{m}_{p}(\mu^{n}_{s}))\right)\psi^{\prime}(|x|^{p})|x|^{p-1}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{s}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}s\\
&\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(|x|^{p})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{0}(x)+C_{1}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(|x|^{p-1}+|x|^{p}\right)\psi^{\prime}(|x|^{p})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{s}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}s\end{split}

for some
C_{1}>0
independent of
n
and
t\in[0,T]
.
Notice that by the monotonicity of
\psi^{\prime}
, denoting by
B_{1}
the unitary ball of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{p-1}\psi^{\prime}(|x|^{p})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{s}(x)&\leq\int_{B_{1}}|x|^{p-1}\psi^{\prime}(|x|^{p})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{s}(x)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B_{1}}|x|^{p}\psi^{\prime}(|x|^{p})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{s}(x)\\
&\leq\psi^{\prime}(1)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{p}\psi^{\prime}(|x|^{p})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{s}(x).\end{split}

By the previous inequality and (
2.8
), from (
6.9
) we get

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(|x|^{p})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{t}(x)&\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(|x|^{p})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{0}(x)+C_{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(1+\psi(|x|^{p})\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{s}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}s\end{split}

for some
C_{2}>0
independent of
n
and
t\in[0,T]
.
By Gronwall’s inequality and (
6.5
) we obtain (
6.6
).

Using Benamou-Brenier formula (
2.7
), the growth condition on
f
in (
3.3
),
for
s,t\in[0,T]
,
s\leq t
, it holds

\begin{split}W^{p}_{p}(\mu^{n}_{s},\mu^{n}_{t})&\leq\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|v^{n}(r,x)|^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{r}(x){\mathrm{d}}r\\
&\leq C\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(1+|x|+\mathrm{m}_{p}(\mu_{r}^{n})\right)^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{r}^{n}(x){\mathrm{d}}r,\end{split}

for some
C>0
independent of
n
,
s
and
t
.
Using the bound (
6.8
) we obtain (
6.7
).

By Ascoli-Arzelà theorem in
\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
there exists
\mu\in C([0,T];\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))

and a subsequence
\mu^{n}
(not relabeled) such that

\mu^{n}\to\mu
in
C([0,T];\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.

For what concerns the weak compactness of
u^{n}
(in the sense of convergence (
6.3
)),
we denote by
\tilde{\mu}^{n}=\mu^{n}_{t}\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\in\mathscr{P}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})

and
\tilde{\mu}=\mu_{t}\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\in\mathscr{P}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
From the convergence of
\mu^{n}
to
\mu
it follows that
\tilde{\mu}^{n}\to\tilde{\mu}
weakly.
Defining

\gamma^{n}:=(i_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}},\underline{u}^{n})_{\sharp}\tilde{\mu}^{n}\in\mathscr{P}(([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\times U)
,
we observe that

\pi^{1}_{\sharp}\gamma^{n}=\tilde{\mu}^{n}\in\mathscr{P}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
and

\pi^{2}_{\sharp}\gamma^{n}=\underline{u}^{n}_{\sharp}\tilde{\mu}^{n}\in\mathscr{P}(U)
, where
\pi^{1}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U\to[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the projection on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\pi^{2}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U\to U
is the projection on
U
.

Since
\pi^{1}_{\sharp}\gamma^{n}
weakly converges and
U
is compact,
the families
\{\pi^{1}_{\sharp}\gamma^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{\pi^{2}_{\sharp}\gamma^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are tight.
Thanks to
[
3
, Lemma 5.2.2]
it follows that
\{\gamma^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight and,
by Prokhorov’s Theorem, there exists
\gamma\in\mathscr{P}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U)
and a subsequence
\gamma^{n}
(not relabeled)
such that,

\gamma^{n}\to\gamma
weakly, as
n\to+\infty
.

Let
\phi\in C_{c}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d};V^{\prime})
.
Recalling that
U
is compact, using the continuous and bounded test function

(t,x,u)\mapsto\langle\phi(t,x),u\rangle
, by the weak convergence of
\gamma^{n}
to
\gamma
we have

(6.10)

\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\langle\phi(t,x),\underline{u}^{n}(t,x)\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{\mu}^{n}(t,x)=\\
&=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{U}\langle\phi(t,x),u\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\gamma^{n}(t,x,u)\to\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{U}\langle\phi(t,x),u\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\gamma(t,x,u),\end{split}

as
n\to+\infty
.
Using Theorem
2.6
(specifically Remark
2.7
) and observing that
\pi^{1}_{\sharp}\gamma=\tilde{\mu}
, we disintegrate
\gamma
with respect to
\pi^{1}
to get

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{U}\langle\phi(t,x),u\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\gamma(t,x,u)=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{U}\langle\phi(t,x),u\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\gamma_{t,x}(u)\,{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{\mu}(t,x).

We define now
\underline{u}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to U
by

(6.11)

\underline{u}(t,x):=\int_{U}u\,{\mathrm{d}}\gamma_{t,x}(u),\qquad\forall\,(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d},

where the integral in (
6.11
) is a Bochner integral.
Since the map
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\gamma_{t,x}\in\mathscr{P}(U)
is a Borel map, then

\underline{u}\in\mathrm{B}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d};U)
.
We call the map
\underline{u}
the barycentric projection of
\gamma
with respect to
\pi^{1}_{\sharp}\gamma
.
Since the Bochner integral commutes with continuous linear functionals, it holds

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{U}\langle\phi(t,x),u\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\gamma_{t,x}(u)\,{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{\mu}(t,x)=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\langle\phi(t,x),\underline{u}(t,x)\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{\mu}(t,x).

Using (
6.10
) we obtain the convergence of
\underline{u}^{n}\to\underline{u}
in the sense of (
6.3
).

In order to prove that
(\mu,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})
we show that (
6.1
) is satisfied.
Let
\varphi\in C^{1}_{c}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Since
(\mu^{n},\underline{u}^{n})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu^{n}_{0})
, for every
t\in[0,T]
it holds

(6.12)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(t,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{t}(x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(0,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{0}(x)=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\partial_{s}\varphi(s,x)+v^{n}(s,x)\cdot\nabla\varphi(s,x)\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{s}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}s.

By the convergence
\mu^{n}\to\mu
in
C([0,T];\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
we immediately pass to the limit, as
n\to+\infty
, in the left hand side of equation (
6.12
) as well as on the term
\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{s}\varphi(s,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{s}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}s
.
Finally, let us rewrite

\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}v^{n}(s,x)\cdot\nabla\varphi(s,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{s}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}s\\
&=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x,\underline{u}^{n}(s,x),\mu^{n}_{s})\cdot\nabla\varphi(s,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{s}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}s\\
&=\int_{[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U}f(x,u,\mu^{n}_{s})\cdot\nabla\varphi(s,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\gamma^{n}(s,x,u)\\
&=\int_{[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U}(f(x,u,\mu^{n}_{s})-f(x,u,\mu_{s}))\cdot\nabla\varphi(s,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\gamma^{n}(s,x,u)\\
&\quad+\int_{[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U}f(x,u,\mu_{s})\cdot\nabla\varphi(s,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\gamma^{n}(s,x,u).\\
\end{split}

By (
3.1
), the first term on the right hand side can be estimated by

\begin{split}&\left|\int_{[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U}\langle f(x,u,\mu^{n}_{s})-f(x,u,\mu_{s}),\nabla\varphi(s,x)\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\gamma^{n}(s,x,u)\right|\\
&\quad\leq L\sup_{r\in[0,T]}W_{p}(\mu^{n}_{r},\mu_{r})\int_{[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U}|\nabla\varphi(s,x)|\,{\mathrm{d}}\gamma^{n}(s,x,u)\\
&\quad=L\sup_{r\in[0,T]}W_{p}(\mu^{n}_{r},\mu_{r})\int_{[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\nabla\varphi(s,x)|\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{s}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}s,\end{split}

which goes to zero as
n\to+\infty
by the convergence
\mu^{n}\to\mu
in
C([0,T];\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.
Hence

\begin{split}\lim_{n\to+\infty}&\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}v^{n}(s,x)\cdot\nabla\varphi(s,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{s}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}s\\
=\lim_{n\to+\infty}&\int_{[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U}f(x,u,\mu_{s})\cdot\nabla\varphi(t,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\gamma^{n}(s,x,u)\\
&=\int_{[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U}f(x,u,\mu_{s})\cdot\nabla\varphi(t,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\gamma(s,x,u),\\
\end{split}

by the weak convergence of
\gamma^{n}
to
\gamma
.
Recall that, by the Convexity Assumption
3.4
, for any
(x,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]
the map
u\mapsto f(x,u,\mu_{s})
is affine.
Thus, using that
\gamma_{s,x}
is a probability measure, we have

\int_{U}f(x,u,\mu_{s})\,{\mathrm{d}}\gamma_{s,x}(u)=f\left(x,\int_{U}u\,{\mathrm{d}}\gamma_{s,x}(u),\mu_{s}\right),\quad\text{ for }\tilde{\mu}\text{-a.e. }(s,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Hence we get

\begin{split}\int_{[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}&\int_{U}f(x,u,\mu_{s})\cdot\nabla\varphi(s,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\gamma_{s,x}(u)\,{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{\mu}(s,x)\\
&=\int_{[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}f\left(x,\int_{U}u\,{\mathrm{d}}\gamma_{s,x}(u),\mu_{s}\right)\cdot\nabla\varphi(s,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{\mu}(s,x)\\
&=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f\left(x,\underline{u}(s,x),\mu_{s}\right)\cdot\nabla\varphi(s,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{s}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}s.\\
\end{split}

Defining
v(t,x):=f(x,\underline{u}(t,x),\mu_{t})
, we have proved that

\begin{split}\lim_{n\to+\infty}&\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}v^{n}(s,x)\cdot\nabla\varphi(s,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{s}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}s\\
&=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}v(s,x)\cdot\nabla\varphi(s,x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{s}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}s\end{split}

and this concludes the proof.
∎

Proposition 6.7

(Lower semicontinuity for convex
J_{\textup{{E}}}
)
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfy the Convexity Assumption
3.4
.
If
(\mu^{n},\underline{u}^{n})
converges to
(\mu,\underline{u})
according to Definition
6.5
, then

(6.13)

\liminf_{n\to+\infty}J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu^{n},\underline{u}^{n})\geq J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu,\underline{u}).

Proof.

Denoting by
\tilde{\mu}^{n}=\mu^{n}_{t}\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\in\mathscr{P}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})

and
\tilde{\mu}=\mu_{t}\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\in\mathscr{P}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
we define
\gamma^{n}:=(i_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}},\underline{u}^{n})_{\sharp}\tilde{\mu}^{n}\in\mathscr{P}(([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\times U)
.
Reasoning as in the proof of Proposition
6.6
, we obtain that there exists

\gamma\in\mathscr{P}(([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\times U)
such that
\pi^{1}_{\sharp}\gamma=\tilde{\mu}

and, up to subsequences,
\gamma^{n}\to\gamma
weakly as
n\to+\infty
.
Moreover, defining
\underline{u}_{b}
by (
6.11
), up to subsequences,

(\mu^{n},\underline{u}^{n})
converges to
(\mu,\underline{u}_{b})
according to Definition
6.5
.
Then,

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\langle\phi(t,x),\underline{u}(t,x)\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{\mu}(t,x)=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\langle\phi(t,x),\underline{u}_{b}(t,x)\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{\mu}(t,x),\quad\forall\,\phi\in C_{c}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d};V^{\prime}),

which implies that
\underline{u}_{b}(t,x)=\underline{u}(t,x)
for
\tilde{\mu}
-a.e.
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Let
{\mathcal{K}}\subset\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a compact set containing

\mu^{n}_{t},\mu_{t}
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in[0,T]
.
Then, for any compact
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, thanks to the continuity of
{\mathcal{C}}
, there exists a modulus of continuity
\alpha:[0,+\infty)\to[0,+\infty)

for the restriction of
{\mathcal{C}}
to the compact set
K\times U\times{\mathcal{K}}
so that

\sup_{(t,x,u)\in[0,T]\times K\times U}|{\mathcal{C}}(x,u,\mu^{n}_{t})-{\mathcal{C}}(x,u,\mu_{t})|\leq\alpha\Big(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}W_{p}(\mu_{t}^{n},\mu_{t})\Big)\to 0\quad\text{as }n\to+\infty.

Then, taking into account that
{\mathcal{C}}\geq 0
, we get

\begin{split}&\liminf_{n\to+\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{C}(x,\underline{u}^{n}(t,x),\mu^{n}_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{t}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\\
&\geq\liminf_{n\to+\infty}\int_{[0,T]\times K\times U}\mathcal{C}(x,u,\mu^{n}_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\gamma^{n}(t,x,u)\\
&\geq\int_{[0,T]\times K\times U}\mathcal{C}(x,u,\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\gamma(t,x,u).\end{split}

Since
K
is arbitrary we obtain

(6.14)

\liminf_{n\to+\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{C}(x,\underline{u}^{n}(t,x),\mu^{n}_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{t}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\geq\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U}\mathcal{C}(x,u,\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\gamma(t,x,u).

Denoting
\gamma_{t,x}
the disintegration of
\gamma
with respect to
\pi^{1}
(as in the proof of Proposition
6.6
), the convexity of the map
u\mapsto{\mathcal{C}}(x,u,\mu_{t})
for any
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}

and Jensen’s inequality yield

\begin{split}\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U}\mathcal{C}(x,u,\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\gamma(t,x,u)&=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{U}\mathcal{C}(x,u,\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\gamma_{t,x}(u)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\\
&\geq\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{C}\left(x,\int_{U}u\,{\mathrm{d}}\gamma_{t,x}(u),\mu_{t}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\\
&=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{C}\left(x,\underline{u}_{b}(t,x),\mu_{t}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\\
&=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{C}\left(x,\underline{u}(t,x),\mu_{t}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}t.\end{split}

By the continuity of
{\mathcal{C}}_{T}
, using the same argument of the proof of
(
6.14
),
we obtain

\begin{split}\liminf_{n\to+\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{C}_{T}(x,\mu^{n}_{T})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{n}_{T}(x)\geq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathcal{C}_{T}(x,\mu_{T})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{T}(x).\end{split}

∎

Propositions
6.6
and
6.7
give immediately the existence of optimizers for our optimal control problem in Eulerian formulation.

Theorem 6.8

(Existence of minimizers for convex
E
)
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfy the Convexity Assumption
3.4
.
If
\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
then there exists
(\mu,\underline{u})\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})

such that

J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu,\underline{u})=V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0}).

As a consequence, we derive the lower semicontinuity of the value function for the Eulerian problem.

Proposition 6.9

(Lower semicontinuity of
V_{\textup{{E}}}
)
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfy the Convexity Assumption
3.4

and
\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
If
\{\mu_{0}^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a sequence such that
W_{p}(\mu_{0}^{n},\mu_{0})\to 0
as
n\to+\infty
,
then

\liminf_{n\to+\infty}V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0}^{n})\geq V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0}).

Proof.

From Theorem
6.8
there exists a sequence
(\mu^{n},\underline{u}^{n})\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0}^{n})

such that
V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0}^{n})=J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu^{n},\underline{u}^{n})
.
By Proposition
6.6
there exists
(\mu,\underline{u})\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})
such that, up to subsequences,

(\mu^{n},\underline{u}^{n})
converges to
(\mu,\underline{u})
according to Definition
6.5
.
Then, using Proposition
6.7
, we have

\liminf_{n\to+\infty}V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0}^{n})=\liminf_{n\to+\infty}J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu^{n},\underline{u}^{n})\geq J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu,\underline{u})\geq V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0}).

∎

7.
Kantorovich optimal control problem and equivalence with the Eulerian

In this section, we provide a further formulation of optimal control problems which we call
Kantorovich formulation
in analogy with the terminology used in optimal transport theory.
This formulation acts as a bridge between the Lagrangian and the Eulerian formulations and it is
based on the representation of solutions of the continuity equation by superposition of continuous curves in

\Gamma_{T}=C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})
(see Theorem
2.5
).
This formulation turns out to be equivalent to the Eulerian one and it will be useful in Section
8
to prove the equivalence between the Eulerian
and the Lagrangian problems.

We recall that, for any
t\in[0,T]
,
e_{t}:\Gamma_{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
denotes the evaluation map
e_{t}(\gamma):=\gamma(t)
.

Definition 7.1

(Kantorovich optimal control problem (
K
))
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1
.
Given
\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we say that
(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0})
if

(i)

u\in\mathrm{B}([0,T]\times\Gamma_{T};U)
;

(ii)

\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\mathscr{P}(\Gamma_{T})
,
(e_{0})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\eta}=\mu_{0}
and,
defining
\mu_{t}:=(e_{t})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\eta}
for all
t\in[0,T]
,

(7.1)

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}t<+\infty.

\boldsymbol{\eta}
is concentrated on the set of absolutely continuous solutions of the differential equation

\dot{\gamma}(t)=f(\gamma(t),u(t,\gamma),\mu_{t}),\qquad\text{for ${\mathcal{L}}_{T}$-a.e. }t\in[0,T].

We define the
cost functional

J_{\textup{{K}}}:\mathscr{P}(\Gamma_{T})\times{\mathrm{B}([0,T]\times\Gamma_{T};U)}\to[0,+\infty]
by

J_{\textup{{K}}}(\boldsymbol{\eta},u):=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma_{T}}{\mathcal{C}}(\gamma(t),u(t,\gamma),\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\eta}(\gamma)\,{\mathrm{d}}t+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{C}}_{T}(x,\mu_{T})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{T}(x),

and the
value function

V_{\textup{{K}}}:\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to[0,+\infty)
by

V_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0}):=\inf\{J_{\textup{{K}}}(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)\,:\,(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0})\}.

Remark 7.2
.

We observe that, by the growth condition of
f
in (
3.3
) and condition (
7.1
),
\boldsymbol{\eta}
is actually concentrated on
\mathrm{AC}^{p}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})
, indeed

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma_{T}}|\dot{\gamma}(t)|^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\eta}(\gamma)\,{\mathrm{d}}t&\leq\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma_{T}}|f(\gamma(t),u(t,\gamma),\mu_{t})|^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\eta}(\gamma)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\\
&\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma_{T}}|1+|\gamma(t)|+\mathrm{m}_{p}(\mu)|^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\eta}(\gamma)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\\
&\leq\tilde{C}\Big(1+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x){\mathrm{d}}t\Big).\end{split}

Hence, by Fubini theorem, for
\boldsymbol{\eta}
-a.e.
\gamma\in\Gamma_{T}
,
\dot{\gamma}\in L^{p}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Moreover, thanks to (
7.1
) and the growth condition (
3.2
),
J_{\textup{{K}}}(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)<+\infty
for every
(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0})
(the proof of
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{p}\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{T}(x)<+\infty

follows by items (i) and (ii) and the same argument used to show (
6.8
) in Proposition
6.6
).

Finally, the value function
V_{\textup{{K}}}
is well defined since
{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{K}}}
is non empty (see Propostion
7.4
).

The aim of this section is to prove the existence of minimizers for the Kantorovich optimal control problem under the Convexity Assumptions
3.4
and to show the equivalence with the Eulerian formulation of the problem described in Section
6
. In particular, we get the equality of the corresponding value functions. This is the content of the following theorem.

Theorem 7.3
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfy the Convexity Assumption
3.4
.
If
\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
there exist
(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0})
and
(\mu,\underline{u})\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})
such that

J_{\textup{{K}}}(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)=J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu,\underline{u})=V_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0})=V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0}).

The proof of Theorem
7.3
follows by the combination of Theorem
6.8
and Propositions
7.4
,
7.5
below.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
\mathbb{S}:=(U,f,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1
.
Let
\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. If
(\mu,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})
, then there exists
(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0})
such that

J_{\textup{{K}}}(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)=J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu,\underline{u})
.
In particular
{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0})\not=\emptyset
and
V_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0})\leq V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})
.

Proof.

Let
(\mu,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})
.
Applying Theorem
2.5
to
\mu
and
v_{t}(x)=f(x,\underline{u}(t,x),\mu_{t})
we get
\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\mathscr{P}(\Gamma_{T})
such that

(e_{t})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\eta}=\mu_{t}
for every
t\in[0,T]
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}
is concentrated on the absolutely continuous solutions of
\dot{\gamma}(t)=v_{t}(\gamma(t))
.
Condition (
7.1
) is automatically satisfied in view of the fact that
(\mu,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})
.
Then, for every
(t,\gamma)\in[0,T]\times\Gamma_{T}
we define
u(t,\gamma):=\underline{u}(t,\gamma(t))
so that
u
is Borel measurable and we have
(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0})
.
Finally, from the evaluation
(e_{t})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\eta}=\mu_{t}
it holds that
J_{\textup{{K}}}(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)=J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu,\underline{u})
.
∎

Under the Convexity Assumption
3.4
it also holds that
V_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0})\geq V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})
.

Proposition 7.5
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfy the Convexity Assumption
3.4
.
Let
\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. If
(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0})
, then there exists

(\mu,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})
such that

J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu,\underline{u})\leq J_{\textup{{K}}}(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)
.
In particular
V_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0})\geq V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})
.
