Proof.

Let
(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0})
. We firstly define
\mu_{t}:=(e_{t})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\eta}
, for every
t\in[0,T]
.
We introduce the continuous evaluation map
e:[0,T]\times\Gamma_{T}\to[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
by setting

e(t,\gamma):=(t,\gamma(t))=(i_{[0,T]}(t),e_{t}(\gamma))

and we denote by
(t,x)\mapsto\tilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{t,x}
the Borel map obtained from the disintegration of
{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\otimes\boldsymbol{\eta}

with respect to
e
, see Theorem
2.6
.
Then we define the function
\underline{u}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to U
by

\underline{u}(t,x):=\int_{[0,T]\times\Gamma_{T}}u(t,\gamma)\,{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{t,x}(\gamma).

Notice that
\underline{u}
is Borel measurable thanks to the Borel measurability of
(t,x)\mapsto\tilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{t,x}
.
The measure
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{t,x}
is concentrated on
\{t\}\times\{\gamma:\gamma(t)=x\}
, so that

\tilde{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{t,x}=\delta_{t}\otimes\boldsymbol{\eta}_{t,x}
, where for any
t\in[0,T]
,
the function
x\mapsto\boldsymbol{\eta}_{t,x}
is the Borel map given by the disintegration of
\boldsymbol{\eta}

with respect to the continuous map
e_{t}
.
Hence we have also that

(7.2)

\underline{u}(t,x)=\int_{\Gamma_{T}}u(t,\gamma)\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{t,x}(\gamma).

Defining the set

A:=\{(t,\gamma)\in[0,T]\times\Gamma_{T}:\exists\,\dot{\gamma}(t)\,\text{ and }\dot{\gamma}(t)=f(\gamma(t),u(t,\gamma),\mu_{t})\}
,
by item (ii) of Definition
7.1
we have that
{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\otimes\boldsymbol{\eta}(([0,T]\times\Gamma_{T})\setminus A)=0
. Then, for
{\mathcal{L}}_{T}
-a.e.
t\in[0,T]
we have

\dot{\gamma}(t)=f(\gamma(t),u(t,\gamma),\mu_{t}),\qquad\text{for $\boldsymbol{\eta}$-a.e. }\gamma\in\Gamma_{T}.

Let
\varphi\in C^{1}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
.
For any
s,t\in[0,T]
,
s<t
, we have

(7.3)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{s}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma_{T}}(\varphi(\gamma(t))-\varphi(\gamma(s)))\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\eta}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma_{T}}\int_{s}^{t}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}r}\varphi(\gamma(r))\,{\mathrm{d}}r\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\eta}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma_{T}}\int_{s}^{t}\nabla\varphi(\gamma(r))\cdot\dot{\gamma}(r)\,{\mathrm{d}}r\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\eta}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma_{T}}\nabla\varphi(\gamma(r))\cdot f(\gamma(r),u(r,\gamma),\mu_{r})\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\eta}(\gamma)\,{\mathrm{d}}r.

Using the growth condition of
f
in (
3.3
) we have

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{s}(x)\right|\leq C\|\nabla\varphi\|_{\infty}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Gamma_{T}}(1+|\gamma(r)|+\mathrm{m}_{p}(\mu_{r}))\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\eta}(\gamma)\,{\mathrm{d}}r.

By (
7.1
) the map
r\mapsto\int_{\Gamma_{T}}(1+|\gamma(r)|+\mathrm{m}_{p}(\mu_{r}))\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\eta}(\gamma)
belongs to
L^{1}(0,T)

and then the map
t\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)
is absolutely continuous.

Thanks to (
7.3
), for
{\mathcal{L}}_{T}
-a.e.
t\in[0,T]
we have

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)=\int_{\Gamma_{T}}\nabla\varphi(\gamma(t))\cdot f(\gamma(t),u(t,\gamma),\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\eta}(\gamma).

Using the affinity of
f
, the disintegration of
\boldsymbol{\eta}
with respect to
e_{t}
,
recalling that
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{t,x}
is concentrated on
\{\gamma:\gamma(t)=x\}
, and by (
7.2
) we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{T}}\nabla\varphi(\gamma(t))\cdot f(\gamma(t),u(t,\gamma),\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\eta}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\Gamma_{T}}\nabla\varphi(\gamma(t))\cdot f(\gamma(t),u(t,\gamma),\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{t,x}(\gamma)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\Gamma_{T}}\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot f(x,u(t,\gamma),\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{t,x}(\gamma)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot f\left(x,\int_{\Gamma_{T}}u(t,\gamma)\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{t,x}(\gamma),\mu_{t}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot f(x,\underline{u}(t,x),\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x).

Then
\mu
satisfies the continuity equation
\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\mathrm{div}(v_{t}\mu_{t})=0
for the vector field

v_{t}(x)=f(x,\underline{u}(t,x),\mu_{t})
in the sense of distributions (see e.g.
[
3
, equation 8.1.4]
).
Since
|v_{t}(x)|^{p}\leq\tilde{C}(1+|x|^{p}+\mathrm{m}_{p}^{p}(\mu_{t}))
, from (
7.1
) it follows that
t\mapsto\|v_{t}\|_{L^{p}_{\mu_{t}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})}

belongs to
L^{p}(0,T)
and then
\mu\in\mathrm{AC}^{p}([0,T];\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.
Hence,
(\mu,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})
.

Finally, by the convexity of
{\mathcal{C}}
with respect to
u
and Jensen’s inequality we obtain

\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma_{T}}{\mathcal{C}}(\gamma(t),u(t,\gamma),\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\eta}(\gamma)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\\
&=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\Gamma_{T}}{\mathcal{C}}(x,u(t,\gamma),\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{t,x}(\gamma){\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\\
&\geq\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{C}}\left(x,\int_{\Gamma_{T}}u(t,\gamma)\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\eta}_{t,x}(\gamma),\mu_{t}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\\
&=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{C}}\left(x,\underline{u}(t,x),\mu_{t}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}t.\end{split}

Hence we obtain
J_{\textup{{K}}}(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)\geq J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu,\underline{u})
.
∎

8.
Equivalence of Eulerian and Lagrangian problems

In this Section we study the equivalence between the Eulerian and Lagrangian formulations of the optimal control problem. We anticipate here the main results of this section.

Theorem 8.1
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfy the Convexity Assumption
3.4
and
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
be a standard Borel space such that
\mathbb{P}
is without atoms.
If
X_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then

V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})=V_{\textup{{E}}}((X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}).

In particular, given
X_{0},X_{0}^{\prime}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
s.t.
(X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=(X_{0}^{\prime})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}
, then

V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})=V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0}^{\prime}).

The proof of Theorem
8.1
follows immediately by Proposition
8.3
, Theorem
8.5
and Theorem
7.3
.

We stress again that the Convexity Assumption
3.4
is sufficient to prove the existence of a minimizer for the Eulerian and Kantorovich optimal control problems (see Theorems
6.8
and
7.3
). However in general, even assuming the Convexity Assumption
3.4
, the Lagrangian optimal control problem could not have minimizers as we show in Section
8.3
.

If we remove the Convexity Assumption
3.4
, we can still give the following equivalence result.

Theorem 8.2
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1
and
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}=(\mathscr{U},\mathscr{F},\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C}_{T})
as in Definition
3.5
.
Let
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
be a standard Borel space such that
\mathbb{P}
is without atoms.
Let
\textup{{L}},\textup{{RL}}
be the Lagrangian and Relaxed Lagrangian problems associated to
\mathbb{S}
.
Let also
\textup{{L}}^{\prime},\textup{{E}}^{\prime}
the Lagrangian and Eulerian problems associated to
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
.
If
X_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then

V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})=V_{\textup{{RL}}}(X_{0})=V_{\textup{{L}}^{\prime}}(X_{0})=V_{\textup{{E}}^{\prime}}((X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P})=V_{\textup{{K}}^{\prime}}((X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}).

The proof of Theorem
8.2
is postponed at the end of Section
8.1
.

8.1.
Comparison between
L
,
E
and
K

We start by comparing the Eulerian and Lagrangian problems under the Convexity Assumption
3.4
.
Assuming
\mathbb{P}
without atoms, we further exhibit the equivalence between the associated value functions exploiting the Kantorovich formulation introduced in Section
7
.

The following is a first comparison between the Eulerian and Lagrangian problems.

Proposition 8.3
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfy the Convexity Assumption
3.4
and
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space.
If
X_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
(X,u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})
, then there exists
(\mu,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}}((X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P})

such that
J_{\textup{{L}}}(X,u)\geq J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu,\underline{u})
. In particular,
V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})\geq V_{\textup{{E}}}((X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P})
.

Proof.

Let
(X,u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})
.
We firstly define
\mu_{t}:=(X_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}
, for every
t\in[0,T]
.
Thanks to Proposition
A.3
in Appendix
A
,
X\in\mathrm{AC}^{p}([0,T];L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
so that

\mu\in\mathrm{AC}^{p}([0,T];\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.

We define
\sigma\in\mathscr{P}([0,T]\times\Omega\times U)
by
\sigma:=\delta_{u(t,\omega)}\otimes\mathbb{P}\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{T}

and
\theta\in\mathscr{P}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U)
by
\theta:=(i_{[0,T]},X_{t},i_{U})_{\sharp}\sigma
.
Let
\pi^{1,2}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U\to[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the projection map
(t,x,u)\mapsto(t,x)
, observe that
\pi^{1,2}_{\sharp}\theta=\mu_{t}\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{T}
.
If we denote
\theta_{t,x}
the disintegration of
\theta
with respect to
\pi^{1,2}
, then we have
\theta=\theta_{t,x}\otimes\mu_{t}\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{T}
.
We define now
\underline{u}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to U
by

(8.1)

\underline{u}(t,x):=\int_{U}u\,{\mathrm{d}}\theta_{t,x}(u).

Thanks to Theorem
2.6
, the map
(t,x)\mapsto\theta_{t,x}
is Borel measurable, so that
\underline{u}\in\mathrm{B}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d};U)
.

The rest of the proof follows the same line of the proof of Proposition
7.5
. We write the details for the reader’s convenience.

Defining the set

A:=\{(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\Omega:\exists\,\dot{X}_{t}(\omega)\text{ and }\dot{X}_{t}(\omega)=f(X_{t}(\omega),u(t,\omega),\mu_{t})\}
, by item (ii) of Definition
4.1
, we have that
{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\otimes\mathbb{P}(([0,T]\times\Omega\setminus A)=0
. Then, for
{\mathcal{L}}_{T}
-a.e.
t\in[0,T]
it holds

\dot{X}_{t}(\omega)=f(X_{t}(\omega),u(t,\omega),\mu_{t}),\qquad\text{for $\mathbb{P}$-a.e. }\omega\in\Omega.

Let
\varphi\in C^{1}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
.
For any
s,t\in[0,T]
,
s<t
, we have

(8.2)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{s}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}(\varphi(X_{t}(\omega))-\varphi(X_{s}(\omega)))\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\int_{s}^{t}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}r}\varphi(X_{r}(\omega))\,{\mathrm{d}}r\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\int_{s}^{t}\nabla\varphi(X_{r}(\omega))\cdot\dot{X}_{r}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}r\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\nabla\varphi(X_{r}(\omega))\cdot f(X_{r}(\omega)),u(r,\omega),\mu_{r})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}r.

Using the growth condition of
f
in (
3.3
) we have

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{s}(x)\right|\leq C\|\nabla\varphi\|_{\infty}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{\Omega}(1+|X_{r}(\omega)|+\mathrm{m}_{p}(\mu_{r}))\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}r.

Thanks to (
4.9
) in Proposition
4.9
, it follows that
the map
r\mapsto\int_{\Omega}(1+|X_{r}(\omega)|+\mathrm{m}_{p}(\mu_{r}))\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)
belongs to
L^{1}(0,T)

so that the map
t\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)
is absolutely continuous.
Then, from (
8.2
) it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)=\int_{\Omega}\nabla\varphi(X_{t}(\omega))\cdot f(X_{t}(\omega)),u(t,\omega),\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega),\quad\text{ for }{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\text{-a.e.}t\in[0,T].

For
{\mathcal{L}}_{T}
-a.e.
t\in[0,T]
, we denote now
\sigma_{t}\in\mathscr{P}(\Omega\times U)
and
\theta_{t}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U)
the disintegrations of
\sigma
and
\theta
with respect to the projection maps
\pi^{1}_{\Omega}:[0,T]\times\Omega\times U\to[0,T]
,
\pi^{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U\to[0,T]
, respectively.
It can be shown that
\theta_{t}=(X_{t},i_{U})_{\sharp}\sigma_{t}=\theta_{t,x}\otimes\mu_{t}
, for
{\mathcal{L}}_{T}
-a.e.
t\in[0,T]
.

Using the affinity of
f
, and the definition of
\underline{u}
in (
8.1
) we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\nabla\varphi(X_{t}(\omega))\cdot f(X_{t}(\omega)),u(t,\omega),\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega\times U}\nabla\varphi(X_{t}(\omega))\cdot f(X_{t}(\omega),u,\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma_{t}(\omega,u)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U}\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot f(x,u,\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\theta_{t}(x,u)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot\int_{U}f(x,u,\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\theta_{t,x}(u)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot f\left(x,\int_{U}u\,{\mathrm{d}}\theta_{t,x}(u),\mu_{t}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot f(x,\underline{u}(t,x),\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x).

Then
\mu
satisfies the continuity equation
\partial_{t}\mu_{t}+\mathrm{div}(v_{t}\mu_{t})=0
for the vector field

v_{t}(x):=f(x,\underline{u}(t,x),\mu_{t})
in the sense of distributions (see e.g.
[
3
, equation (8.1.4)]
).
Since
|v_{t}(x)|^{p}\leq\tilde{C}(1+|x|^{p}+\mathrm{m}_{p}^{p}(\mu_{t}))
, from (
4.9
) it follows that
t\mapsto\|v_{t}\|_{L^{p}_{\mu_{t}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})}

belongs to
L^{p}(0,T)
and
\mu\in\mathrm{AC}^{p}([0,T];\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.
Hence,
(\mu,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})
.

Finally, by the convexity of
{\mathcal{C}}
with respect to
u
and Jensen’s inequality we obtain

\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}{\mathcal{C}}(X_{t}(\omega),u_{t}(\omega),\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\\
&=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega\times U}{\mathcal{C}}(X_{t}(\omega),u,\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\sigma_{t}(\omega,u)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\\
&=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U}{\mathcal{C}}(x,u,\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\theta_{t}(x,u)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\\
&=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{U}{\mathcal{C}}(x,u,\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\theta_{t,x}(u)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\\
&\geq\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{C}}\left(x,\int_{U}u\,{\mathrm{d}}\theta_{t,x}(u),\mu_{t}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x){\mathrm{d}}t\\
&=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{C}}\left(x,\underline{u}(t,x),\mu_{t}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}t.\end{split}

This readily implies that
J_{\textup{{L}}}(X,u)\geq J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu,\underline{u})
.
∎

In the next Lemma, we are given an admissible pair
(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)
for the Kantorovich problem. Considering the evaluation map
Z_{t}(\gamma)=\gamma(t)
, we associate to
(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)
the pair
(Z,u)
which is admissible for the Lagrangian problem with parametrization space
(\Gamma_{T},{\mathcal{B}}_{\Gamma_{T}},\boldsymbol{\eta})
and with the same cost as
(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)
.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1
.
Denote with
Z(t,\gamma):=e_{t}(\gamma)
, for every
t\in[0,T]
and
\gamma\in\Gamma_{T}
.
If
\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0})
and we denote with
\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}=\textup{{L}}(\Gamma_{T},{\mathcal{B}}_{\Gamma_{T}},\boldsymbol{\eta})
, then
(Z,u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(e_{0})
.
Moreover,

J_{\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(Z,u)=J_{\textup{{K}}}(\boldsymbol{\eta},u).

Proof.

Let
(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0})
and denote by
\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}=\textup{{L}}(\Gamma_{T},{\mathcal{B}}_{\Gamma_{T}},\boldsymbol{\eta})
.
Denoting with
Z:[0,T]\times\Gamma_{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
the map defined by

Z(t,\gamma):=\gamma(t)=e_{t}(\gamma)
, let us show that
(Z,u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(e_{0})
.
Since
Z(0,\gamma)_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\eta}=\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
then
e_{0}=Z(0,\cdot)\in L^{p}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\Gamma_{T})
.
By item (i) in Definition
7.1
we have
u\in\mathrm{B}([0,T]\times\Gamma_{T};U)
.
Thanks to (
7.1
) it readily follows that
Z\in L^{p}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\Gamma_{T};L^{p}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and from Remark
7.2
we actually have that
Z\in L^{p}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}(\Gamma_{T};\mathrm{AC}^{p}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.
Moreover, from item (ii) of Definition
7.1
, for
\boldsymbol{\eta}
-a.e.
\gamma\in\Gamma_{T}
, we have

\dot{Z}(t,\gamma)=f(Z(t,\gamma),u(t,\gamma),Z(t,\cdot)_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\eta})\quad\text{ for }{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\text{-a.e.}t\in[0,T].

Hence
(Z,u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(e_{0})
and, by definition of
Z
,
J_{\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(Z,u)=J_{\textup{{K}}}(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)
.

∎

When the parametrization space
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
is fixed a priori, an interesting first comparison between the Kantorovich and Lagrangian problems is given below.

Theorem 8.5
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1
with
U
convex compact subset of a separable Banach space
V
.
Let
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
be a standard Borel space such that

\mathbb{P}
is without atoms.
If
\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0})
, then
for every
X_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
(X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\mu_{0}

there exits a sequence
(X^{n},u^{n})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})
such that

(8.3)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}J_{\textup{{L}}}(X^{n},u^{n})=J_{\textup{{K}}}(\boldsymbol{\eta},u).

Moreover, for every
\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and every
X_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
(X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\mu_{0}
it holds

(8.4)

V_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0})\geq V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0}).

Proof.

Step 1.

Let
(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0})
.
We denote by
\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}:=\textup{{L}}(\Gamma_{T},{\mathcal{B}}_{\Gamma_{T}},\boldsymbol{\eta})
.
Defining

Z(t,\gamma):=\gamma(t)=e_{t}(\gamma)
, by Lemma
8.4
it holds that
(Z,u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(e_{0})
and

(8.5)

J_{\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(Z,u)=J_{\textup{{K}}}(\boldsymbol{\eta},u).

Thanks to the continuity of the evaluation map
e_{0}:\Gamma_{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we apply Proposition
4.22
for the problem
\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
in the Polish space
(\Gamma_{T},{\mathcal{B}}_{\Gamma_{T}},\boldsymbol{\eta})
.
Then there exists a sequence
(\bar{Z}^{m},\bar{u}^{m})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(e_{0})
such that

\bar{Z}^{m}:[0,T]\times\Gamma_{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\bar{u}^{m}:[0,T]\times\Gamma_{T}\to U
are continuous and

(8.6)

\lim_{m\to+\infty}J_{\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(\bar{Z}^{m},\bar{u}^{m})=J_{\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(Z,u).

Step 2.

Let
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
be a standard Borel space such that

\mathbb{P}
is without atoms and
\tau
be a Polish topology on
\Omega
such that
\mathfrak{B}={\mathcal{B}}_{(\Omega,\tau)}
.
Denote with
\textup{{L}}=\textup{{L}}(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}}_{(\Omega,\tau)},\mathbb{P})
the Lagrangian problem for the system
\mathbb{S}
.
Let
X_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
(X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\mu_{0}
.
Given
\bar{Z}\in C([0,T]\times\Gamma_{T};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\bar{u}\in C([0,T]\times\Gamma_{T};U)
such that

(\bar{Z},\bar{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(e_{0})
, let us prove that there exists a sequence

(\tilde{X}^{n},\tilde{u}^{n})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})
such that

(8.7)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}J_{\textup{{L}}}(\tilde{X}^{n},\tilde{u}^{n})=J_{\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(\bar{Z},\bar{u}).

We define the sets

•

\tilde{\Gamma}:=\left\{(x,\gamma)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Gamma_{T}\,:\,x=\gamma(0)=e_{0}(\gamma)\right\};

•

\Gamma_{0}:=\left\{\gamma\in\Gamma_{T}\,:\,\gamma(0)=e_{0}(\gamma)=0\right\},

and the continuous maps

•

r:\tilde{\Gamma}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Gamma_{0}
,
r(x,\gamma):=(x,\gamma-x)
,
where
\gamma-x
is the curve
t\mapsto\gamma(t)-x
.
Notice that
r
admits a left inverse
r^{-1}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Gamma_{0}\to\tilde{\Gamma}
,
r^{-1}(x,\gamma_{0})=(x,\gamma_{0}+x)
,
that obviously satisfies
r^{-1}\circ r=i_{\tilde{\Gamma}}
;

•

s:\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Gamma_{0}\to\Omega\times\tilde{\Gamma}
,
s(\omega,x,\gamma_{0})=(\omega,x,\gamma_{0}+x)
.
Observe that
s=(i_{\Omega},r^{-1})
.

Let us consider the couplings

\rho:=(i_{\Omega},X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}\in\mathscr{P}(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d}),\quad\tilde{\eta}:=(e_{0},i_{\Gamma_{T}})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\mathscr{P}(\tilde{\Gamma}),\quad\hat{\eta}:=r_{\sharp}\tilde{\eta}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Gamma_{0}).

Notice that
\pi^{2}_{\sharp}\rho=\pi^{1}_{\sharp}\tilde{\eta}=\pi^{1}_{\sharp}\hat{\eta}=\mu_{0}
.

We define a measure
\hat{\sigma}\in\mathscr{P}(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Gamma_{0})
satisfying

\pi^{1,2}_{\sharp}\hat{\sigma}=\rho
and
\pi^{2,3}_{\sharp}\hat{\sigma}=\hat{\eta}
.
Since
\mathbb{P}
is without atoms and
\pi^{1}_{\sharp}\rho=\mathbb{P}
then also
\rho\in\mathscr{P}(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
is without atoms.
Applying Lemma
2.10
with
\mathbb{T}=\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
S=\Gamma_{0}
,
\lambda=\rho
and

\nu=\hat{\sigma}
, there exists a sequence of Borel maps
\hat{w}_{n}:\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\Gamma_{0}
such that

(8.8)

\hat{\sigma}_{n}:=(i_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d}},\hat{w}_{n})_{\sharp}\rho\xrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}}\hat{\sigma},\quad\textrm{ as }n\to+\infty.

Define
w_{n}:\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\Gamma_{T}
by
w_{n}(\omega,x):=\hat{w}_{n}(\omega,x)+x
and note that

s(\omega,x,\hat{w}_{n}(\omega,x))=(\omega,x,w_{n}(\omega,x))
.
Thanks to the continuity of
s
, then
s_{\sharp}
is weakly continuous.
From Remark
2.9
, by the composition rule (
2.1
) and (
8.8
), we have that

(8.9)

\tilde{\sigma}_{n}:=s_{\sharp}\hat{\sigma}_{n}=(i_{\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d}},w_{n})_{\sharp}\rho\xrightarrow{\mathcal{Y}}s_{\sharp}\hat{\sigma}=:\tilde{\sigma}\in\mathscr{P}(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Gamma_{T}),\quad\textrm{ as }n\to+\infty.

From (
2.1
), a direct computation shows

\displaystyle\pi^{1,2}_{\sharp}\tilde{\sigma}=\rho,

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}=r^{-1}_{\sharp}\hat{\eta}=\left(r^{-1}\circ\pi^{2,3}\right)_{\sharp}\hat{\sigma}=\left(r^{-1}\circ\pi^{2,3}\circ s^{-1}\right)_{\sharp}\tilde{\sigma}=\pi^{2,3}_{\sharp}\tilde{\sigma}.

We define

\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}:=\pi^{3}_{\sharp}\tilde{\sigma}_{n}\in\mathscr{P}(\Gamma_{T})
.
Observing that
\pi^{3}_{\sharp}\tilde{\sigma}=\boldsymbol{\eta}
, by (
8.9
) we have
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}\to\boldsymbol{\eta}
weakly in
\mathscr{P}(\Gamma_{T})
.
Notice also that
(e_{0})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}=(e_{0}\circ w_{n}\circ(i_{\Omega},X_{0}))_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=(X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\mu_{0}

and
(e_{0})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\eta}=\mu_{0}
. For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, denote by
\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}}:=\textup{{L}}(\Gamma_{T},{\mathcal{B}}_{\Gamma_{T}},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n})
the Lagrangian problem for the system
\mathbb{S}
.
Since
e_{0}
,
\bar{u}
are continuous, we can apply Proposition
4.21
in the probability space

(\Gamma_{T},{\mathcal{B}}_{\Gamma_{T}},\boldsymbol{\eta})
, with
\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n},\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\mathscr{P}(\Gamma_{T})
and initial datum
e_{0}
. Thus if
(Z^{n},\bar{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}}}(e_{0})
, we have that

(8.10)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}J_{\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}}}(Z^{n},\bar{u})=J_{\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(\bar{Z},\bar{u}).

Finally, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the pair
(\tilde{X}^{n},\tilde{u}^{n})
by

\displaystyle\tilde{X}^{n}:[0,T]\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d},\quad

\displaystyle\quad\tilde{X}^{n}(t,\omega):=Z^{n}(t,w_{n}(\omega,X_{0}(\omega)));

\displaystyle\tilde{u}^{n}:[0,T]\times\Omega\to U,\quad

\displaystyle\quad\tilde{u}^{n}(t,\omega):=\bar{u}(t,w_{n}(\omega,X_{0}(\omega))).

Observe that
\tilde{X}^{n}(0,\omega)=Z^{n}(0,w_{n}(\omega,X_{0}(\omega)))=e_{0}(w_{n}(\omega,X_{0}(\omega)))=X_{0}(\omega)
. Moreover, thanks to the composition rule (
2.1
) we have
\pi^{3}_{\sharp}\tilde{\sigma}_{n}=(w_{n})_{\sharp}\rho
so that

(\tilde{X}^{n}_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=(Z^{n}_{t}\circ w_{n}\circ(i_{\Omega},X_{0}))_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=(Z^{n}_{t})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}.

By construction we have
(\tilde{X}^{n},\tilde{u}^{n})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})
and it is immediate to verify that

J_{\textup{{L}}}(\tilde{X}^{n},\tilde{u}^{n})=J_{\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{n}}}(Z^{n},\bar{u}).

Then, by (
8.10
) we obtain (
8.7
).

Step 3.

We apply Step 2 to the sequence
(\bar{Z}^{m},\bar{u}^{m})
constructed in Step 1.
Fix
m\in\mathbb{N}
, then there exists a sequence
(\tilde{X}^{m,n},\tilde{u}^{m,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

(\tilde{X}^{m,n},\tilde{u}^{m,n})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

\lim_{n\to+\infty}J_{\textup{{L}}}(\tilde{X}^{m,n},\tilde{u}^{m,n})=J_{\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(\bar{Z}^{m},\bar{u}^{m}),\qquad\forall\,m\in\mathbb{N}.

Thanks to (
8.6
), by a simple diagonal argument we can select a (not relabelled) sequence

(X^{n},u^{n})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})
satisfying

\lim_{n\to+\infty}J_{\textup{{L}}}(X^{n},u^{n})=J_{\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(Z,u),

where
(Z,u)
are defined in Step 1.
From (
8.5
) we finally get
(
8.3
).

Step 4.

By (
8.3
) and the definition of
V_{\textup{{L}}}
, for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
n_{\varepsilon}>0
such that for
n\geq n_{\varepsilon}

V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})\leq J_{\textup{{L}}}(X^{n},u^{n})\leq J_{\textup{{K}}}(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)+\varepsilon.

From the arbitrariness of
\varepsilon>0
we have

V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})\leq J_{\textup{{K}}}(\boldsymbol{\eta},u),\quad\forall\,(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0}),

hence the required inequality.
∎

Remark 8.6
.

Under the Convexity Assumption
3.4
,
if
(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0})
is an optimal pair, then
(Z,u)
given by Lemma
8.4
is optimal for the Lagrangian problem
\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
.
This is a consequence of Theorem
7.3
, Proposition
8.3
and of (
8.4
) in Theorem
8.5
.

We conclude the section with the proof of Theorem
8.2
.

Proof of Theorem
8.2
.

Thanks to Proposition
3.6
and Remark
5.2

the relaxed Lagrangian problem
RL
in
\mathbb{S}
coincides with
\textup{{L}}^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
.
Precisely, for every
X_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
it holds
{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{RL}}}(X_{0})={\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}^{\prime}}(X_{0})
.
Moreover,
J_{\textup{{RL}}}(X,\sigma)=J_{\textup{{L}}^{\prime}}(X,\sigma)
for every
(X,\sigma)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{RL}}}(X_{0})={\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}^{\prime}}(X_{0})
.
Hence,
V_{\textup{{RL}}}(X_{0})=V_{\textup{{L}}^{\prime}}(X_{0})
.
The equality
V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})=V_{\textup{{RL}}}(X_{0})
follows from Theorem
5.3
.
Finally, Theorem
8.1
yields
V_{\textup{{L}}^{\prime}}(X_{0})=V_{\textup{{E}}^{\prime}}((X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P})=V_{\textup{{K}}^{\prime}}((X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P})
.
∎

8.2.
Continuity of
V_{\textup{{E}}}
,
V_{\textup{{K}}}
and
V_{\textup{{L}}}

Here, we prove continuity results for the value functions of the various proposed formulations.

Theorem 8.7

(Continuity of
V_{\textup{{E}}}
and
V_{\textup{{K}}}
)
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfy the Convexity Assumption
3.4
.
If
\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\{\mu_{0}^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a sequence such that
W_{p}(\mu_{0}^{n},\mu_{0})\to 0
as
n\to+\infty
,
then

\lim_{n\to+\infty}V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0}^{n})=V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0}),\qquad\lim_{n\to+\infty}V_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0}^{n})=V_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0}).

Proof.

Let
\mu_{0}^{n}
converge to
\mu_{0}
in
\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
By Proposition
2.1
with
S=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, there exist
X_{0},X_{0}^{n}\in\mathrm{B}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
(X_{0})_{\sharp}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}=\mu_{0}
,
(X^{n}_{0})_{\sharp}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}=\mu^{n}_{0}
and
X_{0}^{n}(\omega)\to X_{0}(\omega)
for
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
-a.e.
\omega\in[0,1]
.
Since
\mu_{0}^{n},\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have
X_{0}^{n},X_{0}\in L^{p}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Moreover by the convergence
W_{p}(\mu_{0}^{n},\mu_{0})\to 0
and Proposition
2.4
there exists
\psi:[0,+\infty)\to[0,+\infty)
admissible (according to Definition
2.3
)
such that

(8.11)

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{[0,1]}\psi(|X^{n}_{0}(t)|^{p})\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(t)=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(|x|^{p})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{0}^{n}(x)<+\infty.

Thanks to Vitali theorem we get

\|X_{0}^{n}-X_{0}\|_{L^{p}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})}\to 0\quad\text{as }n\to+\infty.

Applying Proposition
4.12
to the Lagrangian problem in
\mathbb{S}
with
(\Omega,{\mathcal{B}},\mathbb{P})=([0,1],{\mathcal{B}}_{[0,1]},{\mathcal{L}}_{1})
we get

\limsup_{n\to+\infty}V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0}^{n})\leq V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})
.
Theorem
8.1
yields

\limsup_{n\to+\infty}V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0}^{n})=\limsup_{n\to+\infty}V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0}^{n})\leq V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})=V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0}).

By Proposition
6.9
we get
\lim_{n\to+\infty}V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0}^{n})=V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})
. Finally, the continuity of
V_{\textup{{K}}}
follows by Theorem
7.3
and the continuity of
V_{\textup{{E}}}
.
∎

Theorem 8.8

(Continuity of
V_{\textup{{L}}}
)
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1
and
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
be a standard Borel space such that
\mathbb{P}
is without atoms.
If
X_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\{X_{0}^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a sequence such that
\|X_{0}^{n}-X_{0}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\to 0
as
n\to+\infty
,
then

\lim_{n\to+\infty}V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0}^{n})=V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0}).

Proof.

From Theorem
8.2
we have
V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0}^{n})=V_{\textup{{E}}^{\prime}}((X_{0}^{n})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P})
and
V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})=V_{\textup{{E}}^{\prime}}((X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P})
.
The application of Theorem
8.7
to
\textup{{E}}^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}=(\mathscr{U},\mathscr{F},\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C}_{T})
(see Definition
3.5
) concludes the proof.
∎

8.3.
A counterexample: Non-existence of minimizers for
L

In the previous sections we have shown that, under the Convexity Assumption
3.4
, the Eulerian and Kantorovich problems always admit a minimizer, see Theorems
6.8
,
7.3
.
This is not always true in the Lagrangian setting.
Existence of minimizers has been shown in Remark
8.6
in the very particular case
\textup{{L}}=\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}
, where
\boldsymbol{\eta}
is optimal for a Kantorovich problem.
In general, for a given parametrization space
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
, the choice of the initial condition is relevant as highlighted in the following.

Theorem 8.9
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfy the Convexity Assumption
3.4
and
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
be a standard Borel space such that
\mathbb{P}
is without atoms.
If
\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
then there exists
X_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
(X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\mu_{0}
and
(X,u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})
such that

(8.12)

J_{\textup{{L}}}(X,u)=V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})=V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0}).

Proof.

Let
\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, by Theorem
7.3
there exists
(\boldsymbol{\eta},\bar{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0})
such that
J_{\textup{{K}}}(\boldsymbol{\eta},\bar{u})=V_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0})
.
Fix
\tau
a Polish topology on
\Omega
such that
\mathfrak{B}={\mathcal{B}}_{(\Omega,\tau)}
.
Since
\mathbb{P}
is without atoms, thanks to Proposition
2.1
there exists a Borel map
\psi:\Omega\to\Gamma_{T}
such that
\psi_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\boldsymbol{\eta}
.
For every
t\in[0,T]
we define
X_{t}:=e_{t}\circ\psi
and the Borel map
u(t,\omega):=\bar{u}(t,\psi(\omega))
.
Using the same techniques as in the proof of Lemma
8.4
we deduce that
(X,u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})
(where
X_{0}:=e_{0}\circ\psi
) and

J_{\textup{{L}}}(X,u)=J_{\textup{{K}}}(\boldsymbol{\eta},\bar{u})
.
By Theorems
8.1
and
7.3
we finally get (
8.12
).
∎

In general, if the initial condition
X_{0}
is assigned a priori, existence of minimizers for the Lagrangian problem is not guaranteed.
We consider the Wasserstein barycenter problem, for which we study the Eulerian and Lagrangian formulations.
In particular, we exhibit an initial datum
X_{0}
whose corresponding Lagrangian problem does not admit minimizers.
We stress that the system under consideration satisfies the Convexity Assumption
3.4
.

8.3.1.
Wasserstein barycenter problem: Eulerian formulation

We consider the setting
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
as follows:
let
U:=\overline{B_{R}(0)}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, for some
R>0
sufficiently large,
T=1
and
p=2
.
We fix
\nu\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with compact support.
We consider the velocity field
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U\times\mathscr{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the cost functions
{\mathcal{C}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U\times\mathscr{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to[0,+\infty)

and
{\mathcal{C}}_{T}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathscr{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to[0,+\infty)
defined by

f(x,u,\mu)=u,\qquad{\mathcal{C}}(x,u,\mu)=|u|^{2},\qquad{\mathcal{C}}_{T}(x,\mu)=W^{2}_{2}(\mu,\nu).

In this setting, the cost functional has the form

J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu,\underline{u})=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\underline{u}(t,x)|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x){\mathrm{d}}t+W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{1},\nu).

For any
\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the associated value function is given by

(8.13)

V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0}):=\inf_{(\mu,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})}J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu,\underline{u}),

and recall that by Theorem
6.8
the infimum in (
8.13
) is actually a minimum.

Let us now fix
\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with compact support and characterize the value function and the corresponding minimizers.
By the Benamou-Brenier formula (
2.7
), we have the lower bound

(8.14)

\begin{split}\inf_{(\mu,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\underline{u}(t,x)|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x){\mathrm{d}}t+W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{1},\nu)\right)\geq\inf_{\mu_{1}\in\mathscr{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\left[W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})+W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{1},\nu)\right].\end{split}

Using the triangle inequality, it is easy to prove that

(8.15)

W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})+W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{1},\nu)\geq\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\nu),\qquad\forall\,\mu_{1}\in\mathscr{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

and, for any constant speed Wasserstein geodesic
\{\sigma_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
such that
\sigma_{0}=\mu_{0}
and
\sigma_{1}=\nu
, the measure

\mu_{1}=\sigma_{1/2}
realizes the equality in (
8.15
).
Since the supports of
\mu_{0}
and
\nu
are compact, then the support of
\sigma_{1/2}
is compact and, denoting by

\{\mu_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
a Wasserstein geodesic joining
\mu_{0}
to
\sigma_{1/2}
,
we also have that a vector field
\underline{u}
realizing the equality

(8.16)

\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\underline{u}(t,x)|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x){\mathrm{d}}t=W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\sigma_{1/2})

is bounded (see e.g.
[
51
, Section 5.4]
).
Then, using
\underline{u}
satisfying (
8.16
) and choosing
R
sufficiently large, we obtain the equality in (
8.14
).
The value of the minimum is

(8.17)

V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})=W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\sigma_{1/2})+W_{2}^{2}(\sigma_{1/2},\nu)=\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\nu).

Notice that the minimizer
(\mu,\underline{u})
is not unique a priori.
If at least one of the measures
\nu
and
\mu_{0}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
{\mathcal{L}}^{d}
,
then the geodesic
\{\sigma_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
is unique and the map
\eta\mapsto W^{2}_{2}(\eta,\nu)
is strictly convex.
In this case
\sigma_{1/2}
is the unique minimizer of the functional
\eta\mapsto W^{2}_{2}(\mu_{0},\eta)+W^{2}_{2}(\eta,\nu)

and the pair
(\mu,\underline{u})
, with
\mu_{t}:=\sigma_{t/2}
for all
t\in[0,1]
, is the (unique) minimizer for the Eulerian problem.

8.3.2.
Wasserstein barycenter problem: Lagrangian formulation

Let
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
be a standard Borel space such that
\mathbb{P}\in\mathscr{P}(\Omega)
is without atoms.
The Lagrangian cost functional of the Wasserstein barycenter problem is given by

(8.18)

J_{\textup{{L}}}(X,u):=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\Omega}|u_{t}(\omega)|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega){\mathrm{d}}t+W_{2}^{2}((X_{1})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P},\nu).

For any
X_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the corresponding value function is

V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})=\inf_{(X,u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})}J_{\textup{{L}}}(X,u).

Since
(X,u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})
satisfies, for
\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
, the system

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\dot{X}_{t}(\omega)=u_{t}(\omega),\qquad\text{ for }{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\text{-a.e. }t\in(0,1)\\
X|_{t=0}(\omega)={X_{0}(\omega)},\end{array}\right.

we have

(8.19)

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{1}&\int_{\Omega}|u_{t}(\omega)|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega){\mathrm{d}}t\geq\int_{\Omega}\left|\int_{0}^{1}u_{t}(\omega){\mathrm{d}}t\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)\\
&=\int_{\Omega}\left|\int_{0}^{1}\dot{X}_{t}(\omega){\mathrm{d}}t\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)=\int_{\Omega}\left|X_{1}(\omega)-X_{0}(\omega)\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)\end{split}

where we have applied Fubini theorem and Jensen’s inequality.
Notice that the inequality in (
8.19
) becomes an equality if
(X,u)
belongs to the restrict admissibility class given by

\begin{split}\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0}):=\{(X,u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})\,:\;u_{t}(\omega)=\bar{u}(\omega),\,\forall t\in[0,1],\text{ for $\mathbb{P}$-a.e. }\omega\in\Omega\}.\end{split}

Suppose now that
\mu_{0}:=(X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}
has compact support (i.e.
X_{0}
bounded),
then we can compare the Lagrangian and Eulerian formulation of the Wasserstein barycenter problem.
Indeed, for
R
sufficiently large we have

(8.20)

\begin{split}V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})&=\inf_{(X,\bar{u})\in\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})}\left(\int_{\Omega}|X_{1}(\omega)-X_{0}(\omega)|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)+W_{2}^{2}((X_{1})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P},\nu)\right)\\
=V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})&=\min_{\mu_{1}\in\mathscr{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\left(W^{2}_{2}(\mu_{0},\mu_{1})+W^{2}_{2}(\mu_{1},\nu)\right)=\frac{1}{2}W_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0},\nu).\end{split}

where the first equality follows by the choice
(X,\bar{u})\in\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})
, the second equality is given by Theorem
8.1
and the last two equalities are exactly (
8.17
).

We now exhibit an example where the infimum for the Lagrangian problem is not a minimum.
Let us consider
\Omega=[0,1]
,
\mathbb{P}={\mathcal{L}}_{1}
and fix the dimension
d=2
.

We set
\nu:={\mathcal{L}}^{2}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}[0,1]^{2}
,

X_{0}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
defined by
X_{0}(\omega)=(1/2,\omega)
.
We observe that
\mu_{0}:=(X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\mathscr{H}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(\{1/2\}\times[0,1])
.
We also notice that
X_{0}^{-1}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to[0,1]
,
defined
\mu_{0}
-a.e. has the form
X_{0}^{-1}(1/2,\omega)=\omega
.

Since

\begin{split}\int_{[0,1]}\left|X_{1}(\omega)-X_{0}(\omega)\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|X_{1}(X_{0}^{-1}(x))-x\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{0}(x),\end{split}

and defining

(8.21)

\begin{split}\mathscr{B}_{\mu_{0}}&:=\{X_{1}\circ X_{0}^{-1}:X_{1}=X_{0}+\bar{u}\,\text{ and }(X,\bar{u})\in\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})\}\\
&=\{Y\in L^{2}((\mathbb{R}^{2},\mu_{0});\mathbb{R}^{2}):|Y(x)-x|\leq R,\text{ for $\mu_{0}$-a.e. }x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\},\end{split}

we easily get

(8.22)

\begin{split}V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})&=\inf_{(X,\bar{u})\in\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})}J_{\textup{{L}}}(X,\bar{u})\\
&=\inf_{Y\in\mathscr{B}_{\mu_{0}}}\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|Y(x)-x\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{0}(x)+W^{2}_{2}(Y_{\sharp}\mu_{0},\nu)\right].\end{split}

As already observed at the end of subsection
8.3.1
,
since
\nu
is absolutely continuous with respect to
{\mathcal{L}}^{2}
, there exists a unique geodesic
\{\sigma_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
joining
\mu_{0}
to
\nu
.
Furthermore,
\sigma_{1/2}
is the unique minimizer of the functional
\eta\mapsto W^{2}_{2}(\mu_{0},\eta)+W^{2}_{2}(\eta,\nu)
.
Then, for
R
sufficiently large, from (
8.20
) and (
8.22
) we know that

(8.23)

\begin{split}W^{2}_{2}(\mu_{0},\sigma_{1/2})+W^{2}_{2}(\sigma_{1/2},\nu)&=\inf_{Y\in\mathscr{B}_{\mu_{0}}}\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|Y(x)-x\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{0}(x)+W^{2}_{2}(Y_{\sharp}\mu_{0},\nu)\right]\\
&\leq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}Y\in\mathscr{B}_{\mu_{0}}\\
Y_{\sharp}\mu_{0}=\sigma_{1/2}\end{subarray}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|Y(x)-x\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{0}(x)+W^{2}_{2}(\sigma_{1/2},\nu).\end{split}

On the other hand, since
\mathop{\rm supp}\nolimits(\sigma_{1/2})\subset[0,1]^{2}
, by
[
49
, Theorem B]
we have

(8.24)

W^{2}_{2}(\mu_{0},\sigma_{1/2})=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}Y\in\mathscr{B}_{\mu_{0}}\\
Y_{\sharp}\mu_{0}=\sigma_{1/2}\end{subarray}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|Y(x)-x\right|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{0}(x)

and, consequently, equality holds in (
8.23
). Moreover the infimum in (
8.24
) is not attained.
Indeed, the map
T:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
defined by
T(x_{1},x_{2})=(1/2,x_{2})
satisfies
T_{\sharp}\nu=\mu_{0}
and

T=\nabla\varphi
for
\varphi(x_{1},x_{2})=\frac{1}{2}(x_{1}+x_{2}^{2})
, therefore
T
is the optimal transport map from
\nu
to
\mu_{0}
.
The unique geodesic joining
\mu_{0}
to
\nu
is
\sigma_{t}=(t(x_{1},x_{2})+(1-t)(1/2,x_{2}))_{\sharp}\nu
and

\sigma_{1/2}=(\frac{1}{2}x_{1}+\frac{1}{4},x_{2}))_{\sharp}\nu
coincides with the uniform probability measure on
[1/4,3/4]\times[0,1]
.
The map
T
is still the optimal transport map from
\sigma_{1/2}
to
\mu_{0}
and the unique optimal transport plan between

\sigma_{1/2}
and
\mu_{0}
is
((x_{1},x_{2}),(1/2,x_{2}))_{\sharp}\sigma_{1/2}
. Then the unique optimal transport plan between

\mu_{0}
and
\sigma_{1/2}
is
\gamma:=((1/2,x_{2}),(x_{1},x_{2}))_{\sharp}\sigma_{1/2}
.
Since
\gamma
is not concentrated on the graph of a map, the optimal transport map from
\mu_{0}
to
\sigma_{1/2}
does not exist.

Since (
8.24
) has not minimizers, then (
8.22
) cannot have minimizers.
Indeed, suppose there exists a minimizer
(X,u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})
for
J_{\textup{{L}}}
in (
8.22
).
Then
X_{1}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfies
(X_{1})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}=\sigma_{1/2}
,
u=X_{1}-X_{0}
and
X_{t}=tu+X_{0}
.
Defining
Y=X_{1}\circ X_{0}^{-1}
, we have that
Y_{\sharp}\mu_{0}=\sigma_{1/2}
so that

Y
is a minimizer in (
8.24
), which is absurd.

Remark 8.10
.

Notice that existence of minimizers is not guaranteed even for Relaxed Lagrangian problems.
Indeed, the same results obtained for the Wasserstein barycenter problem in
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
given in Section
8.3.1
, can be easily extended to the lifted system
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}=(\mathscr{U},\mathscr{F},\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C}_{T})
associated to
\mathbb{S}
(see Definition
3.5
).

In the proposed example, the Lagrangian and Eulerian problems
\textup{{L}}^{\prime},\textup{{E}}^{\prime}
associated to
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
can be treated as the problems
\textup{{L}},\textup{{E}}
associated to
\mathbb{S}

thanks to the following simple observation:
given a probability measure
\rho\in\mathscr{P}(U)
, by Jensen’s inequality we have

\int_{U}|u|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}\rho(u)\geq\left|\int_{U}u\,{\mathrm{d}}\rho(u)\right|^{2},

and the equality holds if and only if
\rho=\delta_{\underline{u}}
for some
\underline{u}\in U
. This guarantees that possible control minimizers for
\textup{{L}}^{\prime},\textup{{E}}^{\prime}
are of the form
\delta_{\underline{u}}
with
\underline{u}

non-relaxed
control for
\textup{{L}},\textup{{E}}
, respectively.
The corresponding trajectories for
\textup{{L}}^{\prime},\textup{{E}}^{\prime}
with control
\delta_{\underline{u}}
coincide with the ones associated to
\underline{u}
for problems
L
and
E
, respectively.
Finally, thanks to Remark
5.2
, non-existence of minima for
\textup{{L}}^{\prime}
corresponds to non-existence of minima for
RL
.

9.
Finite particle systems and Gamma-convergence

To model the evolution of a finite number of particles, we introduce a discrete finite space
\Omega^{N}
with the corresponding normalized counting measure
\mathbb{P}^{N}
.
In this setting, in order to prove equivalence between Eulerian and Lagrangian problems, we cannot directly apply the results given in Theorems
8.1
due to the requirement on the probability measure
\mathbb{P}
to be without atoms (see in particular Theorem
8.5
).
Hence, we introduce a further formulation of the Lagrangian problem in the context of feedback controls (see Definition
9.5
) and we exploit a discrete formulation of the superposition principle for which we refer to Theorem
C.1
.

Furthermore, in Subsections
9.2
and
9.3
, we prove a (discrete to continuous)
\Gamma
-convergence result respectively for the Lagrangian and Eulerian cost functionals when the number of particles goes to infinity.

9.1.
Equivalences between
N
-particles problems

Let
(\Omega^{N},\mathcal{S}(\Omega^{N}),\mathbb{P}^{N})
given by

(9.1)

\displaystyle\Omega^{N}:=\{1,\dots,N\},\quad\mathcal{S}(\Omega^{N}):=\sigma(\{1\},\ldots\{N\})),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{N}(\{k\}):=\frac{1}{N},\quad k=1,\dots,{N}.

We will refer to
\mathbb{P}^{N}
as the normalized counting measure, which can be written as

\mathbb{P}^{N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\delta_{k}.

Let us denote with
\textup{{L}}^{N}=\textup{{L}}(\Omega^{N},\mathcal{S}(\Omega^{N}),\mathbb{P}^{N})
the Lagrangian problem associated to the probability space
(\Omega^{N},\mathcal{S}(\Omega^{N}),\mathbb{P}^{N})
.
Notice that the functional space
L^{p}(\Omega^{N};\mathbb{R}^{d})
coincides with the space of all maps
g:\Omega^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which can be identified with
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N}
.

Differently from the Lagrangian problem
\textup{{L}}^{N}
, where we just need to fix the parametrization space, the definition of the
N
-particle Eulerian problem requires the introduction of a further constraint.
Let us firstly define the subspace of
\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
given by the discrete measures as

(9.2)

\mathscr{P}^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):=\left\{\mu=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}\;\text{ for some }x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\right\}.

Definition 9.1

(Discrete Eulerian optimal control problem
(\textup{{E}}^{N})
)
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1
. Given
\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we say that

(\mu,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu_{0})
, if

(i)

(\mu,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})
;

(ii)

\mu_{t}\in\mathscr{P}^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, for every
t\in[0,T]
.

We define the
cost functional

J_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}:=J_{\textup{{E}}}
and the
value function

V_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu_{0}):=\inf\{J_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu,\underline{u}):(\mu,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu_{0})\}.

Remark 9.2
.

Notice that item (ii) in Definition
9.1
does not follow from the requirement
\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Indeed,
the control map
\underline{u}
in general is not Lipschitz continuous so that uniqueness of characteristics is not guaranteed.

Observe that, for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, it holds

(9.3)

V_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu_{0})\geq V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0}),\qquad\forall\,\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

The main result of this section is given in the following theorem.

Theorem 9.3
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1
.
Let
p\geq 1
and
X_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega^{N};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then

V_{\textup{{L}}^{N}}(X_{0})=V_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}((X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}^{N}).

The proof is a direct consequence of Propositions
9.4
,
9.7
and
9.8
below.

Exploiting the argument contained in
[
34
, Lemma 6.2]
we derive a first comparison between
V_{\textup{{L}}^{N}}(X_{0})
and
V_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}((X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}^{N})
.

Proposition 9.4
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1
and let
X_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega^{N};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. If
(X,u)\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{L}}^{N}}(X_{0})
, then there exists

(\mu,\underline{u})\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}((X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}^{N})
such that
J_{\textup{{L}}^{N}}(X,u)\geq J_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu,\underline{u})
. Moreover,
V_{\textup{{L}}^{N}}(X_{0})\geq V_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}((X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}^{N})
.

Proof.

Let
(X,u)\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{L}}^{N}}(X_{0})
.
Let us define
\mathcal{X}(t):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:X_{t}(\omega)=x\text{ for some }\omega\in\Omega^{N}\}
and

(9.4)

J(t,x):=\{\omega\in\Omega^{N}:X_{t}(\omega)=x\},\qquad\text{ for any }\,(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d},

and denote by
\mathcal{P}
the collection of partitions
P
of
\Omega^{N}
.
It is clear that the family
P_{X}(t):=\{J(t,x):x\in\mathcal{X}(t)\}
belongs to
\mathcal{P}
.
As proved in
[
34
, Lemma 6.2]
, there exists a finite partition on Borel sets of the interval
[0,T]

of the form
\{S_{P}:P\in\mathcal{P}\}
, where
S_{P}:=\{t\in[0,T]:P_{X}(t)=P\}
.

Given
\omega,\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega^{N}
and a Borel set
S\subset[0,T]
, if
X_{t}(\omega)=X_{t}(\omega^{\prime})
for any
t\in S

then, by the absolute continuity of the curves
t\mapsto X_{t}(\omega)
and
t\mapsto X_{t}(\omega^{\prime})
,
we have
\dot{X}_{t}(\omega)=\dot{X}_{t}(\omega^{\prime})
, for
{\mathcal{L}}_{T}
-a.e.
t\in S
.

We define
\mu_{t}:=(X_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}^{N}
and we observe that

(9.5)

\mu_{t}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}(t)}\#J(t,x)\delta_{x}.

Moreover, defining for any
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}

\bar{\omega}_{t,x}\in\mathop{\rm argmin}\limits_{\omega\in J(t,x)}{\mathcal{C}}(x,u(t,\omega),\mu_{t}),

we set

(9.6)

\underline{u}(t,x):=u(t,\bar{\omega}_{t,x}).

We show that
(\mu,\underline{u})\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}((X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}^{N})
.
Let us fix
\varphi\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
For
{\mathcal{L}}_{T}
-a.e.
t\in[0,T]
it holds

(9.7)

\begin{split}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}((X_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}^{N})(x)&=\int_{\Omega^{N}}\nabla\varphi(X_{t}(\omega))\cdot f(X_{t}(\omega),u(t,\omega),\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}^{N}(\omega)\\
&=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\omega=1}^{N}\nabla\varphi(X_{t}(\omega))\cdot f(X_{t}(\omega),u(t,\omega),\mu_{t})\\
&=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}(t)}\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot\sum_{\omega\in J(t,x)}f(x,u(t,\omega),\mu_{t}).\end{split}

Since
f(x,u(t,\omega),\mu_{t})=f(x,\underline{u}(t,x),\mu_{t})
for every
\omega\in J(t,x)
, we can rewrite the right hand side of (
9.7
) to get

\begin{split}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}(t)}\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot\sum_{\omega\in J(t,x)}f(x,u(t,\omega),\mu_{t})&=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}(t)}\#J(t,x)\,\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot f(x,\underline{u}(t,x),\mu_{t})\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot f(x,\underline{u}(t,x),\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x),\end{split}

where the last equality is a consequence of (
9.5
).

For what concerns the cost functional, we have

\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}{\mathcal{C}}(X_{t}(\omega),u(t,\omega),\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}^{N}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}t\\
&\quad=\frac{1}{N}\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}(t)}\sum_{\omega\in J(t,x)}{\mathcal{C}}(x,u(t,\omega),\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}t\\
&\quad\geq\frac{1}{N}\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}(t)}{\#J(t,x)}{\mathcal{C}}(x,\underline{u}(t,x),\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}t\\
&\quad=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{C}}(x,\underline{u}(t,x),\mu_{t})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}t,\end{split}

where the inequality comes from the definition of
\underline{u}
in (
9.6
) and the last equality follows from (
9.5
).
Since

\int_{\Omega^{N}}{\mathcal{C}}_{T}(X_{T}(\omega),\mu_{T})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}^{N}(\omega)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{C}}_{T}(x,\mu_{T})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{T}(x),

we conclude that
J_{\textup{{L}}^{N}}(X,u)\geq J_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu,\underline{u})
.
∎

Here we introduce a feedback formulation of the Lagrangian optimal control problem in order to prove the reverse inequality
V_{\textup{{L}}^{N}}(X_{0})\leq V_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}((X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}^{N})
.
We firstly show its relation with the Lagrangian and Eulerian problems in a general context, i.e. where the probability space
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
is not necessarily the space
(\Omega^{N},\mathcal{S}(\Omega^{N}),\mathbb{P}^{N})
associated to the
N
-particles framework.

Definition 9.5

(Feedback Lagrangian optimal control problem
(FL)
)
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1
and let
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space. Given
X_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we say that

(X,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{FL}}}(X_{0})

if

(i)

\underline{u}\in\mathrm{B}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d};U)
;

(ii)

X\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathrm{AC}^{p}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and for

\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
,
X(\omega)
is a
solution of the following Cauchy problem

(9.8)

\begin{cases}\dot{X}_{t}(\omega)=f(X_{t}(\omega),\underline{u}_{t}(X_{t}(\omega)),(X_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}),&\text{ for }{\mathcal{L}}_{T}\text{-a.e. }t\in]0,T]\\
X_{|t=0}(\omega)=X_{0}(\omega),&\end{cases}

where
X_{t}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is defined by
X_{t}(\omega):=X(t,\omega)
for
\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
.

We refer to
(X,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{FL}}}(X_{0})
as to an
admissible pair
, with
X
a
trajectory
and
\underline{u}
a
feedback control
.

We define the cost functional
J_{\textup{{FL}}}:L^{p}(\Omega;C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times{\mathrm{B}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d};U)}\to[0,+\infty)

by

J_{\textup{{FL}}}(X,\underline{u}):=\int_{\Omega}\int_{0}^{T}{\mathcal{C}}(X_{t}(\omega),\underline{u}_{t}(X_{t}(\omega)),(X_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P})\,{\mathrm{d}}t\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega)+\int_{\Omega}{\mathcal{C}}_{T}(X_{T}(\omega),(X_{T})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega),

and the corresponding
value function

V_{\textup{{FL}}}:L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})\to[0,+\infty)
by

(9.9)

V_{\textup{{FL}}}(X_{0}):=\inf\left\{J_{\textup{{FL}}}(X,\underline{u})\,:\,(X,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{FL}}}(X_{0})\right\}.

In the following,
\textup{{FL}}(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
denotes the Feedback Lagrangian problem given in Definition
9.5
.
We short the notation to
FL
when the probability space is clear from the context.

Remark 9.6
.

Observe that, choosing a constant (feedback) control function
\underline{u}
, from Proposition
4.8

it is immediate to prove that
{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{FL}}}(X_{0})\not=\emptyset
.
In general, given
\underline{u}\in\mathrm{B}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d};U)
, the existence and uniqueness of solutions to the
Cauchy problem (
9.8
) is not guaranteed.

The following result follows directly from Definitions
4.1
,
6.1
and
9.5
.

Proposition 9.7
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1
and
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space.
Let
X_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
. If
(X,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{FL}}}(X_{0})
, then

(i)

defining
u:[0,T]\times\Omega\to U
by
u(t,\omega):=\underline{u}(t,X_{t}(\omega))
,
we have that
(X,u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})
and
J_{\textup{{FL}}}(X,\underline{u})=J_{\textup{{L}}}(X,u)
. In particular it holds
V_{\textup{{FL}}}(X_{0})\geq V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})
.

(ii)

defining
\mu_{t}:=(X_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}
for any
t\in[0,T]
,
we have that
(\mu,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}}((X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P})

and
J_{\textup{{FL}}}(X,\underline{u})=J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu,\underline{u})
.
In particular it holds
V_{\textup{{FL}}}(X_{0})\geq V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})
.

Taking advantage of Proposition
9.7
and of the discrete superposition principle given in Theorem
C.1
, we have the following equivalence result between
\textup{{FL}}^{N}:=\textup{{FL}}(\Omega^{N},\mathcal{S}(\Omega^{N}),\mathbb{P}^{N})
and
\textup{{E}}^{N}
defined in Definition
9.1
.

Proposition 9.8
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1
.
Let
p\geq 1
,
X_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega^{N};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\mu_{0}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\omega=1}^{N}\delta_{X_{0}(\omega)}=(X_{0})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}^{N}
.
If
(\mu,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu_{0})
,
then there exists
X\in C([0,T];L^{p}(\Omega^{N}))

such that
\mu_{t}=(X_{t})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}^{N}
for all
t\in[0,T]
,

(X,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{FL}}^{N}}(X_{0})
and

J_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu,\underline{u})=J_{\textup{{FL}}^{N}}(X,\underline{u})
.
Moreover,
V_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu_{0})=V_{\textup{{FL}}^{N}}(X_{0})
.

Proof.

Let
(\mu,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu_{0})
.
By the superposition principle given in Theorem
C.1
applied to
\mu
and the vector field
v(t,x)=f(x,\underline{u}(t,x),\mu_{t})
,
there exists
\eta\in\mathscr{P}(\Gamma_{T})
such that

\eta=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\omega=1}^{N}\delta_{\gamma_{\omega}},

where
\gamma_{\omega}\in\Gamma_{T}
,
\omega=1,\ldots,N
.
We define
X:[0,T]\times\Omega^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

X(t,\omega):=\gamma_{\omega}(t)
that satisfies
(X,u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{FL}}^{N}}
thanks to (
C.4
) in Theorem
C.1
.
Hence it readily follows that
J_{\textup{{FL}}^{N}}(X,\underline{u})=J_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu,\underline{u})
and
V_{\textup{{FL}}^{N}}(X_{0})\leq V_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu_{0})
.
By Proposition
9.7
we have that
V_{\textup{{FL}}^{N}}(X_{0})\geq V_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu_{0})
and we conclude the proof.
∎

9.2.
Finite particle approximation for
L

The aim of this section is to approximate a general Lagrangian problem
\textup{{L}}=\textup{{L}}(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
with finite particle Lagrangian problems
\textup{{L}}^{N}=\textup{{L}}(\Omega^{N},\mathcal{S}(\Omega^{N}),\mathbb{P}^{N})
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
, where
(\Omega^{N},\mathcal{S}(\Omega^{N}),\mathbb{P}^{N})
is defined in (
9.1
).
A first result in this direction has been already obtained in Proposition
4.18
(see also Remark
4.19
) in Section
4.2
.
Here, we specialize the result in the case of equally distributed masses which is suitable for the application to a finite particle/agent model.

Recall that if
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
is a standard Borel space and
\mathbb{P}
is without atoms, thanks to item (ii) in Proposition
4.16
there exists a family of finite algebras
\mathfrak{B}^{N}\subset\mathfrak{B}
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
, satisfying the finite approximation property of Definition
4.15
and

\mathbb{P}(A^{N}_{k})=\frac{1}{N}
, with
k=1,\ldots,N
.
Recall the definition of
\psi^{N}
,
\phi^{N}
and
{\mathcal{K}}^{N}
in (
4.22
) and (
4.23
), respectively.

A Gamma-convergence result for the functional
J_{\textup{{L}}}
is given in the following proposition.

Proposition 9.9

(Finite particle approximation for
L
)
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1
.
Let
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
be a standard Borel space such that

\mathbb{P}
is without atoms.
The following holds:

(i)

Suppose that
(X,u)\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathrm{AC}^{p}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times\mathrm{M}([0,T]\times\Omega;U)
and

(X^{N},u^{N})\in C([0,T];L^{p}(\Omega^{N};\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times\mathrm{M}([0,T]\times\Omega^{N};U)
such that

\lim_{N\to+\infty}{\mathcal{K}}^{N}(X^{N},u^{N})=(X,u),\quad\text{ in }C([0,T];L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times L^{1}([0,T]\times\Omega;U).

Then

\lim_{N\to+\infty}J_{\textup{{L}}^{N}}(X^{N},u^{N})=J_{\textup{{L}}}(X,u).

(ii)

Assume that
U
is a compact convex subset of a separable Banach space
V
.
Suppose that
X_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
(X,u)\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})
.
If
X_{0}^{N}\in L^{p}(\Omega^{N};\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

(9.10)

\lim_{N\to+\infty}\|X^{N}_{0}\circ\psi^{N}-X_{0}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}=0

then there exists a sequence

(X^{N},u^{N})\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{L}}^{N}}(X^{N}_{0})
such that

\lim_{N\to+\infty}{\mathcal{K}}^{N}(X^{N},u^{N})=(X,u),\quad\text{ in }C([0,T];L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times L^{1}([0,T]\times\Omega;U)

and

\lim_{N\to+\infty}J_{\textup{{L}}^{N}}(X^{N},u^{N})=J_{\textup{{L}}}(X,u).

Proof.

Thanks to Proposition
A.3
it holds that
X\in C([0,T];L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.
Item (i) can be proved exactly by the same technique used in the second part of the proof of Proposition
4.11
applied to the sequence
{\mathcal{K}}^{N}(X^{N},u^{N})
and recalling that
J_{\textup{{L}}}\left({\mathcal{K}}^{N}(X^{N},u^{N})\right)=J_{\textup{{L}}^{N}}(X^{N},u^{N})
(see Proposition
4.14
).
Notice that, since
U
is metrizable and compact the convergence
u^{N}\to u
in
L^{1}([0,T]\times\Omega;U)
is equivalent to the convergence in
({\mathcal{L}}_{T}\otimes\mathbb{P})
-measure.
Item (ii), is a direct application of Proposition
4.18
to the sequence of finite algebras
\mathfrak{B}^{N}
given in item (ii) of Proposition
4.16
.
∎

Proposition 9.10

(Convergence of the value functions)
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfy Assumption
3.1
with
U
a compact convex subset of a separable Banach space
V
.
Let
(\Omega,\mathfrak{B},\mathbb{P})
be a standard Borel space such that
\mathbb{P}
is
without atoms.
If
X_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
X_{0}^{N}\in L^{p}(\Omega^{N};\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfy

(9.11)

\lim_{N\to+\infty}\|X^{N}_{0}\circ\psi^{N}-X_{0}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})}=0,

then

\limsup_{N\to+\infty}V_{\textup{{L}}^{N}}(X_{0}^{N})\leq V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0}).

Moreover, if
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfies the Convexity Assumption
3.4
, then

\liminf_{N\to+\infty}V_{\textup{{L}}^{N}}(X_{0}^{N})\geq V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0}).

In particular, under the Convexity Assumption
3.4
,

\lim_{N\to+\infty}V_{\textup{{L}}^{N}}(X_{0}^{N})=V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0}).

Proof.

By definition of inf, for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
(X_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon})\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})
such that
V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})\geq J_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon})-\varepsilon
. Moreover from item (ii) in Proposition
9.9
there exists
(X^{N}_{\varepsilon},u^{N}_{\varepsilon})
such that
J_{\textup{{L}}^{N}}(X^{N}_{\varepsilon},u^{N}_{\varepsilon})\to J_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon})
, as
N\to+\infty
. Hence

\limsup_{N\to+\infty}V_{\textup{{L}}^{N}}(X_{0}^{N})\leq\limsup_{N\to+\infty}J_{\textup{{L}}^{N}}(X^{N}_{\varepsilon},u^{N}_{\varepsilon})=J_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{\varepsilon},u_{\varepsilon})\leq V_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0})+\varepsilon.

By the arbitrariness of
\varepsilon
we conclude.

By Proposition
9.4
and (
9.3
) we get

V_{\textup{{L}}^{N}}(X_{0}^{N})\geq V_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu_{0}^{N})\geq V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0}^{N}).

In the convex setting, by the lower semicontinuity of the value function
V_{\textup{{E}}}
(see Proposition
6.9
)
and by Corollary
8.1
(
\mathbb{P}
is without atoms by assumption) we have the desired convergence.
∎

Remark 9.11
.

A natural choice for
X^{N}_{0}
in Proposition
9.10
is given by
X_{0}^{N}:=\tilde{X}^{N}_{0}\circ\phi^{N}
where

\tilde{X}^{N}_{0}:=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\mathds{1}_{A_{k}^{N}}\,\fint_{A_{k}^{N}}X_{0}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathbb{P}(\omega).

For a proof of the convergence (
9.11
) we refer to Lemma
D.3
and Proposition
D.1
in Appendix
D
.

9.3.
Finite particle approximation for
E

In this section, we show that the Eulerian problem
E
can be approximated by finite particle Eulerian problems
\textup{{E}}^{N}
defined in Definition
9.1
.
Thanks to Theorem
9.3
, we are able to approximate the Eulerian problem also with a sequence of finite particle Lagrangian problems
\textup{{L}}^{N}=\textup{{L}}(\Omega^{N},\mathcal{S}(\Omega^{N}),\mathbb{P}^{N})
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
, with
(\Omega^{N},\mathcal{S}(\Omega^{N}),\mathbb{P}^{N})
as in (
9.1
). This is relevant from the point of view of applications.
The main result of the section is stated in the following theorem.

Theorem 9.12

(Convergence of the value functions)
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{T})
satisfy the Convexity Assumption
3.4
.
Let
\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\mu_{0}^{N}\in\mathscr{P}^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

such that
W_{p}(\mu_{0}^{N},\mu_{0})\to 0
as
N\to+\infty
,
then

\lim_{N\to+\infty}V_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu_{0}^{N})=V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0}).

Moreover, for every
X_{0}^{N}\in L^{p}(\Omega^{N};\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
(X_{0}^{N})_{\sharp}\mathbb{P}^{N}=\mu_{0}^{N}
it holds that

\lim_{N\to+\infty}V_{\textup{{L}}^{N}}(X_{0}^{N})=V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0}).

In order to prove Theorem
9.12
, we start with the following proposition.

Proposition 9.13

(Finite particle approximation for
E
)
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(U,f,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{C}}_{T})
satisfy the Convexity Assumption
3.4
.
Let
\mu_{0}\in\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\mu_{0}^{N}\in\mathscr{P}^{N}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

N\in\mathbb{N}
, such that

(9.12)

\lim_{N\to+\infty}W_{p}(\mu_{0}^{N},\mu_{0})=0.

If
(\mu,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})
,
then there exists a sequence
(\mu^{N},\underline{u}^{N})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu_{0}^{N})
such that

(1)

(\mu^{N},\underline{u}^{N})
converges to
(\mu,\underline{u})
according to Definition
6.5
;

(2)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to+\infty}J_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu^{N},\underline{u}^{N})=J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu,\underline{u})
.

Proof.

Step 1.

Let
(\mu,\underline{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})
.
In this step we associate to
(\mu,\underline{u})
an admissible pair
(\tilde{X},\tilde{u})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(\tilde{X}_{0})
for the Lagrangian problem
\textup{{L}}:=\textup{{L}}([0,1];{\mathcal{B}}_{[0,1]},{\mathcal{L}}_{1})
such that

(9.13)

J_{\textup{{L}}}(\tilde{X},\tilde{u})=J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu,\underline{u}).

By Proposition
7.4
, there exists
(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{K}}}(\mu_{0})
such that

u(t,\gamma)=\underline{u}(t,\gamma(t))
,
(e_{t})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\eta}=\mu_{t}

and
J_{\textup{{K}}}(\boldsymbol{\eta},u)=J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu,\underline{u})
.
Thanks to Lemma
8.4
,
the map
Z:[0,T]\times\Gamma_{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
defined by

Z(t,\gamma):=\gamma(t)=e_{t}(\gamma)
satisfies
(Z,u)\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(e_{0})
with
\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}:=\textup{{L}}(\Gamma_{T},{\mathcal{B}}_{\Gamma_{T}},\boldsymbol{\eta})
and

(9.14)

J_{\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(Z,u)=J_{\textup{{K}}}(\boldsymbol{\eta},u),

By Proposition
2.1
applied to
S=\Gamma_{T}
and
\nu=\boldsymbol{\eta}
, there exists a Borel map
P:[0,1]\to\Gamma_{T}
such that
P_{\sharp}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}=\boldsymbol{\eta}
.

We define

\tilde{X}:[0,T]\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
by
\tilde{X}_{t}(\omega):=Z_{t}(P(\omega))

and
\tilde{u}:[0,T]\times[0,1]\to U
by
\tilde{u}_{t}(\omega):=u_{t}(P(\omega))
.
Notice that

(9.15)

(\tilde{X}_{t})_{\sharp}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}=(e_{t}\circ P)_{\sharp}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}=(e_{t})_{\sharp}\boldsymbol{\eta}=\mu_{t},

and it is easy to prove that

(\tilde{X},\tilde{u})\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{L}}}(\tilde{X}_{0})
.
Moreover
J_{\textup{{L}}}(\tilde{X},\tilde{u})=J_{\textup{{L}}_{\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(Z,u)

and, by (
9.14
), we obtain (
9.13
).

Step 2.

We use the partition of
[0,1]
defined in Lemma
D.3
.
We define the piecewise constant initial data
\tilde{X}_{0}^{N}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
controls
\tilde{u}^{N}:[0,T]\times[0,1]\to U
by

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{0}^{N}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\mathds{1}_{I^{N}_{k}}\,\fint_{I^{N}_{k}}\tilde{X}_{0}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\omega),

\displaystyle\tilde{u}_{t}^{N}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\mathds{1}_{I^{N}_{k}}\,\fint_{I^{N}_{k}}\tilde{u}_{t}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\omega)\,,\quad\textrm{for all }t\in[0,T].

From the definition of
\tilde{X}_{0}^{N}
and Lemma
D.3
we have

(9.16)

W_{p}(\tilde{\mu}_{0}^{N},\mu_{0})\leq\|\tilde{X}_{0}^{N}-\tilde{X}_{0}\|_{L^{p}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})}\to 0\qquad\text{as }N\to+\infty,

where

\tilde{\mu}_{0}^{N}:=(\tilde{X}_{0}^{N})_{\sharp}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\delta_{\tilde{x}^{N}_{k}},\qquad\tilde{x}^{N}_{k}:=\fint_{I^{N}_{k}}\tilde{X}_{0}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Since
\mu_{0}^{N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\delta_{x^{N}_{k}}
,
there exists a permutation of indexes
\sigma^{N}:\{1,\dots,N\}\to\{1,\dots,N\}
and a map
X_{0}^{N}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
defined by
X_{0}^{N}:=\sum_{k=1}^{N}x^{N}_{\sigma^{N}(k)}\mathds{1}_{I^{N}_{k}}
such that

(9.17)

W_{p}(\mu_{0}^{N},\tilde{\mu}_{0}^{N})=\|X_{0}^{N}-\tilde{X}_{0}^{N}\|_{L^{p}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Using (
9.12
), (
9.16
) and (
9.17
) we obtain

(9.18)

\begin{split}\|X_{0}^{N}-\tilde{X}_{0}\|_{L^{p}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})}&\leq\|X_{0}^{N}-\tilde{X}^{N}_{0}\|_{L^{p}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|\tilde{X}_{0}^{N}-\tilde{X}_{0}\|_{L^{p}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})}\\
&=W_{p}(\mu_{0}^{N},\tilde{\mu}_{0}^{N})+\|\tilde{X}_{0}^{N}-\tilde{X}_{0}\|_{L^{p}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})}\\
&\leq W_{p}(\mu_{0}^{N},\mu_{0})+W_{p}(\mu_{0},\tilde{\mu}_{0}^{N})+\|\tilde{X}_{0}^{N}-\tilde{X}_{0}\|_{L^{p}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})}\to 0.\end{split}

Let
(X^{N},\tilde{u}^{N})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{L}}}(X_{0}^{N})
.
By Lemma
D.3
and dominated convergence we have that

\|\tilde{u}^{N}-\tilde{u}\|_{L^{1}([0,1]\times[0,T];V)}\to 0
as
N\to+\infty
.
Then, by Proposition
4.11
, we have

(9.19)

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|X_{t}^{N}-\tilde{X}_{t}\|_{L^{p}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{d})}\to 0\qquad\mbox{ as }N\to+\infty

and

(9.20)

\lim_{N\to+\infty}J_{\textup{{L}}}(X^{N},\tilde{u}^{N})=J_{\textup{{L}}}(\tilde{X},\tilde{u}).

We observe that, for any
t\in[0,T]
,
X_{t}^{N}
is constant on the elements
I_{k}^{N}

of partition
\{I_{k}^{N}:k=1,\ldots,N\}
so that it is of the form

X_{t}^{N}:=\sum_{k=1}^{N}(x_{t}^{N})_{k}\mathds{1}_{I^{N}_{k}}

for some
(x_{t}^{N})_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

We define
\mu_{t}^{N}:=(X_{t}^{N})_{\sharp}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\in\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
From the observation above,

\mu_{t}^{N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\delta_{(x_{t}^{N})_{k}}

By (
9.19
) we obtain that
\mu^{N}\to\mu
in
C([0,T];\mathscr{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
as
N\to+\infty
.

Step 3.

For any
t\in[0,T]
we define
\rho_{t}^{N}:=(X_{t}^{N},\tilde{u}_{t}^{N})_{\sharp}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U)
, and notice that

\pi^{1}_{\sharp}\rho_{t}^{N}=\mu_{t}^{N}
.
Denoting by
\rho_{t,x}^{N}\in\mathscr{P}(U)
the disintegration of
\rho_{t}^{N}
w.r.t.
\pi^{1}
,
we define the Borel map
\underline{u}^{N}:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to U
by

\underline{u}^{N}(t,x):=\int_{U}u\,{\mathrm{d}}\rho_{t,x}^{N}(u).

By the definition of
\mu_{t}^{N}
and
\underline{u}^{N}
, from item (2) of the Convexity Assumption
3.4
,
we obtain that
(\mu^{N},\underline{u}^{N})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu_{0}^{N})
.
Indeed, given
\varphi\in C^{1}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
, for
{\mathcal{L}}_{T}
-a.e.
t\in[0,T]
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}^{N}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\int_{[0,1]}\varphi(X_{t}^{N}(\omega))\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,1]}\nabla\varphi(X_{t}^{N}(\omega))\cdot\dot{X}_{t}^{N}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathcal{L}}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,1]}\nabla\varphi(X_{t}^{N}(\omega))\cdot f(X_{t}^{N}(\omega),\tilde{u}_{t}^{N}(\omega),\mu_{t}^{N})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{L}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U}\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot f(x,u,\mu_{t}^{N})\,{\mathrm{d}}\rho_{t}^{N}(x,u)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot\int_{U}f(x,u,\mu_{t}^{N})\,{\mathrm{d}}\rho_{t,x}^{N}(u)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}^{N}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot f\left(x,\int_{U}u\,{\mathrm{d}}\rho_{t,x}^{N}(u),\mu_{t}^{N}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}^{N}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\nabla\varphi(x)\cdot f\left(x,\underline{u}^{N}(t,x),\mu_{t}^{N}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}^{N}(x).

Let us conclude the proof of the convergence showing that (
6.3
) holds.
For any
t\in[0,T]
, using
(\tilde{X},\tilde{u})
introduced in Step 1, we define
\rho_{t}:=(\tilde{X}_{t},\tilde{u}_{t})_{\sharp}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U)
.
By the convergence (
9.19
) and the convergence of
\tilde{u}^{N}
to
\tilde{u}
, it easily follows that

\rho^{N}:=\rho^{N}_{t}\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{T}
weakly converges to
\rho:=\rho_{t}\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{T}
in
\mathscr{P}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U)
.

Let now
\phi\in C_{c}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d};V^{\prime})
. Using the definition of
\rho^{N}
and the weak convergence of
\rho^{N}
to
\rho
we have that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to+\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\langle\phi(t,x),\bar{u}^{N}(t,x)\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}^{N}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to+\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\langle\phi(t,x),\int_{U}u\,{\mathrm{d}}\rho^{N}_{t,x}(u)\right\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{N}_{t}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to+\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{U}\left\langle\phi(t,x),u\right\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\rho^{N}_{t,x}(u)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{N}_{t}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to+\infty}\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U}\langle\phi(t,x),u\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\rho^{N}(t,x,u),

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U}\langle\phi(t,x),u\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\rho(t,x,u),

Using
\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\mathscr{P}(C([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,
P:[0,1]\to\Gamma_{T}
and (
9.15
) introduced in Step 1, recalling that
\rho=(\tilde{X}_{t},\tilde{u}_{t})_{\sharp}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{T}
we get

\displaystyle\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U}\langle\phi(t,x),u\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\rho(t,x,u)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{1}\langle\phi(t,\tilde{X}_{t}(\omega)),\tilde{u}_{t}(\omega)\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{1}\langle\phi(t,e_{t}(P(\omega))),u_{t}(P(\omega))\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma_{T}}\langle\phi(t,e_{t}(\gamma)),u_{t}(\gamma)\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\eta}(\gamma)\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma_{T}}\langle\phi(t,e_{t}(\gamma)),\underline{u}(t,e_{t}(\gamma))\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\eta}(\gamma)\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\langle\phi(t,x),\underline{u}(t,x)\rangle\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}t.

Step 4.

Finally we prove that
J_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu^{N},\underline{u}^{N})\to J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu,\underline{u})
, as
N\to+\infty
.

Using item (3) of the Convexity Assumption
3.4
, we have

\displaystyle J_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu^{N},\underline{u}^{N})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{C}}(x,\bar{u}^{N}(t,x),\mu_{t}^{N})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu^{N}_{t}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}t+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{C}}_{T}(x,\mu_{T}^{N})\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{T}^{N}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathcal{C}}\left(x,\int_{U}u\,{\mathrm{d}}\rho_{t,x}^{N}(u),\mu_{t}^{N}\right)\,{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}^{N}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}t+\int_{[0,1]}{\mathcal{C}}_{T}(X_{T}^{N}(\omega),\mu_{T}^{N})\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\omega)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times U}{\mathcal{C}}(x,u,\mu_{t}^{N})\,{\mathrm{d}}\rho_{t}^{N}(x,u)\,{\mathrm{d}}t+\int_{[0,1]}{\mathcal{C}}_{T}(X_{T}^{N}(\omega),\mu_{T}^{N})\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{[0,1]}{\mathcal{C}}(X_{t}^{N}(\omega),\tilde{u}_{t}^{N}(\omega),\mu_{t}^{N})\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\omega)\,{\mathrm{d}}t+\int_{[0,1]}{\mathcal{C}}_{T}(X_{T}^{N}(\omega),\mu_{T}^{N})\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\omega)

\displaystyle=J_{\textup{{L}}}(X^{N},\tilde{u}^{N}).

By the previous inequality, recalling (
9.20
) and (
9.13
), we get

\limsup_{N\to+\infty}J_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu^{N},\underline{u}^{N})\leq\limsup_{N\to+\infty}J_{\textup{{L}}}(X^{N},\tilde{u}^{N})=J_{\textup{{L}}}(\tilde{X},\tilde{u})=J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu,\underline{u}).

By Proposition
6.7
we conclude.
∎

We conclude this section with the proof of Theorem
9.12
.

Proof of Theorem
9.12
.

Observe that
V_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu_{0}^{N})\geq V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0}^{N})
, then by Proposition
6.9
we obtain

\liminf_{N\to+\infty}V_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu_{0}^{N})\geq V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0}).

Let us prove that
\limsup_{N\to+\infty}V_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu_{0}^{N})\leq V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})
.
Fix
\varepsilon>0
. By definition of
V_{\textup{{E}}}
there exists

(\mu_{\varepsilon},\underline{u}_{\varepsilon})\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})
such that
V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})\geq J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{\varepsilon},\underline{u}_{\varepsilon})-\varepsilon
.
By Proposition
9.13
there exists
(\mu^{N}_{\varepsilon},\underline{u}^{N}_{\varepsilon})\in\mathcal{A}_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu_{0}^{N})
such that
\lim_{N\to+\infty}J_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu^{N}_{\varepsilon},\underline{u}^{N}_{\varepsilon})=J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{\varepsilon},\underline{u}_{\varepsilon})
. Thus,

\limsup_{N\to+\infty}V_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu_{0}^{N})\leq\limsup_{N\to+\infty}J_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu^{N}_{\varepsilon},\underline{u}^{N}_{\varepsilon})=J_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{\varepsilon},\underline{u}_{\varepsilon})\leq V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})+\varepsilon.

By the arbitrariness of
\varepsilon
we conclude.

Finally, thanks to Theorem
9.3
it holds that
V_{\textup{{E}}^{N}}(\mu_{0}^{N})=V_{\textup{{L}}^{N}}(X_{0}^{N})
for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, so that
\lim_{N\to+\infty}V_{\textup{{L}}^{N}}(X_{0}^{N})=V_{\textup{{E}}}(\mu_{0})
.
∎

Appendixes

We organize the material of the appendixes as follows.
Appendix
A
deals with vector-valued Sobolev spaces and Cauchy problems for ODEs in Banach spaces.
A further stability property of Cauchy problems is then established in Appendix
B
.
In Appendix
C
we state and prove the superposition principle for the evolution of empirical measures.
Appendix
D
is devoted to the proof of Proposition
4.16
where we construct (equipartite) finite algebras satisfying the Finite Approximation Property of Definition
4.15
.

Appendix A
Ordinary differential equations in Banach spaces

Let
E
be a Banach space with
\|\cdot\|
the associated norm.
In the following, if
u:[0,T]\to E
is a Bochner integrable function,
we denote by
\int_{0}^{T}u(t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t
its Bochner integral.
We recall the following criterion of integrability:

u:[0,T]\to E
is Bochner integrable if and only if there exists a sequence

u_{n}:[0,T]\to E
of simple measurable functions such that

\lim_{n\to+\infty}u_{n}(t)=u(t)
for
{\mathcal{L}}_{T}
-a.e.
t\in[0,T]
,
and

\int_{0}^{T}\|u(t)\|\,{\mathrm{d}}t<+\infty
.

We recall that, if
u:[0,T]\to E
